Accordingly, the [Simla Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simla_Agreement) was formulated and signed by the two countries, whereby the countries resolved to settle their differences by peaceful means through bilateral negotiations and to maintain the sanctity of the Line of Control. Multilateral negotiations were not ruled out, but they were conditional upon both sides agreeing to them.[^cite_note-Cohen_2002-188]<sup>: 49–50 </sup> To India, this meant an end to the UN or other multilateral negotiations. However Pakistan reinterpreted the wording in the light of a reference to the "UN charter" in the agreement, and maintained that it could still approach the UN. The United States, United Kingdom and most Western governments agree with India's interpretation.[^cite_note-189]

The Simla Agreement also stated that the two sides would meet again for establishing durable peace. Reportedly Bhutto asked for time to prepare the people of Pakistan and the National Assembly for a final settlement. Indian commentators state that he reneged on the promise. Bhutto told the National Assembly on 14 July that he forged an equal agreement from an unequal beginning and that he did not compromise on the right of self-determination for Jammu and Kashmir. The envisioned meeting never occurred.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGuha,_India_after_Gandhi2008Sec. 20.VII-190]

### Kargil War (1999)



Location of starting the war

Main article: [Kargil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kargil_War)

In mid-1999, alleged insurgents and Pakistani soldiers from [Pakistani Kashmir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Areas,_Pakistan) infiltrated Jammu and Kashmir. During the winter season, Indian forces regularly move down to lower altitudes, as severe climatic conditions makes it almost impossible for them to guard the high peaks near the Line of Control. This practice is followed by both India and Pakistan Army. The terrain makes it difficult for both sides to maintain a strict border control over Line of Control. The insurgents took advantage of this and occupied vacant mountain peaks in the [Kargil range](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kargil_district) overlooking the highway in Indian Kashmir that connects [Srinagar](294148.md) and [Leh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leh). By blocking the highway, they could cut off the only link between the Kashmir Valley and [Ladakh](303611.md). This resulted in a large-scale conflict between the Indian and Pakistani armies. The final stage involved major battles by Indian and Pakistani forces, with India recapturing most of the territories[^cite_note-191][^cite_note-192] held by Pakistani forces.

Fears of the [Kargil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kargil_War) turning into a [nuclear war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_warfare) provoked the then-United States President [Bill Clinton](3356.md) to pressure Pakistan to retreat. The Pakistan Army withdrew their remaining troops from the area, ending the conflict. India regained control of the Kargil peaks, which they now patrol and monitor all year long.

## Internal conflict

See also: [Timeline of the Kashmir conflict](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_Kashmir_conflict)

### Political movements during the Dogra rule (1846–1947)

Main article: [Political movements in Jammu and Kashmir (princely state)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_movements_in_Jammu_and_Kashmir_\(princely_state\))

See also: [Timeline of the Kashmir conflict § 1846–1945: Princely state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_Kashmir_conflict#1846%E2%80%931945:_Princely_state)

In 1932 [Sheikh Abdullah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sheikh_Abdullah), a Kashmiri, and [Chaudhry Ghulam Abbas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chaudhry_Ghulam_Abbas), a Jammuite, led the founding of the *[All-Jammu and Kashmir Muslim Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/All_Jammu_and_Kashmir_Muslim_Conference)* in order to agitate for the rights of Muslims in the state.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBehera,_Demystifying_Kashmir200716-193] In 1938, they renamed the party [National Conference](512306.md) in order to make it representative of all Kashmiris independent of religion.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGuha,_Opening_a_Window_in_Kashmir200480-194][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPuri,_Across_the_Line_of_Control201316-195] The move brought Abdullah closer to [Jawaharlal Nehru](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jawaharlal_Nehru), the rising leader of the Congress party.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchofield,_Kashmir_in_Conflict200321-196] The National Conference eventually became a leading member of the *[All-India States Peoples' Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/All_India_States_Peoples'_Conference)*, a Congress-sponsored confederation of the political movements in the princely states.

Three years later, rifts developed within the Conference owing to political, regional and ideological differences. A faction of the party's leadership grew disenchanted with Abdullah's leanings towards Nehru and the Congress, and his secularisation of Kashmiri politics.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESnedden,_Kashmir_The_Unwritten_History201323-197][^cite_note-Low1991-198][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBehera,_Demystifying_Kashmir200719-199][^cite_note-Snedden2015-200] Consequently, Abbas broke away from the National Conference and revived the old [Muslim Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/All_Jammu_and_Kashmir_Muslim_Conference) in 1941, in collaboration with [Mirwaiz Yusuf Shah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mirwaiz_Yusuf_Shah). These developments indicated fissures between the ethnic [Kashmiris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kashmiris) and Jammuites, as well as between the Hindus and Muslims of Jammu.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPuri,_Across_the_Line_of_Control201316–17-201] Muslims in the Jammu region were Punjabi-speaking and felt closer affinity to [Punjabi Muslims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punjabi_Muslims) than with the Valley Kashmiris.[^cite_note-202] In due course, the Muslim Conference started aligning itself ideologically with the [All-India Muslim League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/All-India_Muslim_League), and supported its [call for an independent 'Pakistan'](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lahore_Resolution).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESnedden,_Kashmir_The_Unwritten_History201323-197] The Muslim Conference derived popular support among the Muslims of the Jammu region, and some from the Valley.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchofield,_Kashmir_in_Conflict200322–23-203][^cite_note-FOOTNOTESnedden,_Kashmir_The_Unwritten_History201324-204] Conversely, Abdullah's National Conference enjoyed influence in the Valley.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESnedden,_Kashmir_The_Unwritten_History201324-204] [Chitralekha Zutshi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chitralekha_Zutshi) states that the political loyalties of Valley Kashmiris were divided in 1947, but the Muslim Conference failed to capitalise on it due its fractiousness and the lack of a distinct political programme.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZutshi,_Languages_of_Belonging2004299-205]

In 1946, the National Conference launched the 'Quit Kashmir' movement, asking the Maharaja to hand the power over to the people. The movement came under criticism from the Muslim Conference, who charged that Abdullah was doing it to boost his own popularity, waning because of his pro-India stance. Instead, the Muslim Conference launched a 'campaign of action' similar to Muslim League's programme in British India. Both Abdullah and Abbas were imprisoned.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchofield,_Kashmir_in_Conflict200324-206] By 22 July 1947, the Muslim Conference started calling for the state's accession to Pakistan.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESnedden,_Kashmir_The_Unwritten_History201325-207]

The [Dogra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dogras) Hindus of Jammu were originally organised under the banner of *All Jammu and Kashmir Rajya Hindu Sabha*, with [Prem Nath Dogra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prem_Nath_Dogra) as a leading member.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPuri,_The_Question_of_Accession20104-208] In 1942, [Balraj Madhok](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balraj_Madhok) arrived in the state as a [pracharak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RSS_Pracharak) of the [Rashtriya Swayamsevak Sangh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rashtriya_Swayamsevak_Sangh) (RSS). He established branches of the RSS in Jammu and later in the Kashmir Valley. Prem Nath Dogra was also the chairman (*sanghchalak*) of the RSS in Jammu.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJaffrelot,_Religion,_Caste_and_Politics2011288,_301-209][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJaffrelot,_Hindu_Nationalist_Movement1996149–150-210] In May 1947, following the Partition plan, the Hindu Sabha threw in its support to whatever the Maharaja might decide regarding the state's status, which in effect meant support for the state's independence. However, following the communal upheaval of the Partition and the tribal invasion, its position changed to supporting the accession of the state to India and, subsequently, full integration of Jammu with India.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPuri,_The_Question_of_Accession20104–5-211][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGupta,_Jammu_and_Kashmir2012194–195-212] In November 1947, shortly after the state's accession to India, the Hindu leaders launched the [Jammu Praja Parishad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jammu_Praja_Parishad) with the objective of achieving the "full integration" of Jammu and Kashmir with India, opposing the "communist-dominated anti-Dogra government of Sheikh Abdullah."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJaffrelot,_Religion,_Caste_and_Politics2011288,_301-209][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGupta,_Jammu_and_Kashmir2012195-213]

### Autonomy and plebiscite (1947–1953)

[Article 370](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Article_370) was drafted in the [Indian constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_constitution) granting special autonomous status to the state of [Jammu and Kashmir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jammu_and_Kashmir_\(state\)), as per [Instrument of Accession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Instrument_of_Accession_\(Jammu_and_Kashmir\)). This article specifies that the State must concur in the application of laws by Indian parliament, except those that pertain to Communications, Defence and Foreign Affairs. Central Government could not exercise its power to interfere in any other areas of governance of the state.

In a broadcast on 2 November 1947, Prime Minister Jawaharlal Nehru announced that the fate of Kashmir would ultimately be decided by the people, once law and order was established, through a referendum "held under international auspices like the United Nations."[^cite_note-Ray2007-214] A similar pledge was made by the Government of India when the Kashmir dispute was referred to the UN Security Council on 1 January 1948.[^cite_note-Ray2007-214] By some accounts Mountbatten had an understanding with Nehru that a referendum on the region's future would be held later.[^cite_note-Wynbrandt2009-215]

[Sheikh Abdullah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sheikh_Abdullah) took oath as [Prime Minister of the state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Chief_Ministers_of_Jammu_and_Kashmir) on 17 March 1948. In 1949, the Indian government obliged Hari Singh to leave Jammu and Kashmir and yield the government to Sheikh Abdullah. [Karan Singh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karan_Singh), the son of the erstwhile [Maharajah Hari Singh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hari_Singh) was made the *[Sadr-i-Riyasat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sadr-i-Riyasat)* (Constitutional [Head of State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Head_of_State)) and the Governor of the state.

[Elections were held](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1951_Jammu_and_Kashmir_Constituent_Assembly_election) for the [Constituent Assembly of Jammu and Kashmir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constituent_Assembly_of_Jammu_and_Kashmir) in 1951, with 75 seats allocated for the Indian administered part of Kashmir, and 25 seats left reserved for the Pakistan administered part. Sheikh Abdullah's National Conference won all 75 seats in a [rigged election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1951_Jammu_and_Kashmir_Constituent_Assembly_election#The_election).[^cite_note-216][^cite_note-\:10-217] In October 1951, [Jammu & Kashmir National Conference](512306.md) under the leadership of Sheikh Abdullah formed the [Constituent Assembly of Jammu and Kashmir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constituent_Assembly_of_Jammu_and_Kashmir) to formulate the Constitution of the state. Sheikh initially wanted the Constituent Assembly to decide the State's accession. But this was not agreed to by Nehru, who stated that such "underhand dealing" would be very bad, as the matter was being decided by the UN.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERaghavan,_War_and_Peace_in_Modern_India2010190–191-218]

Sheikh Abdullah was said to have ruled the state in an undemocratic and authoritarian manner during this period.[^cite_note-\:9-219]

According to historian Zutshi, in the late 1940s, most Kashmiri Muslims in Indian Kashmir were still debating the value of the state's association with India or Pakistan. By the 1950s, she says, the National Conference government's repressive measures and the Indian state's seeming determination to settle the state's accession to India without a reference to the people of the state brought Kashmiri Muslims to extol the virtues of Pakistan and condemn India's high-handedness in its occupation of the territory, and even those who had been in India's favour began to speak in terms of the state's association with Pakistan.[^cite_note-220]

In early 1949, an [agitation was started](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jammu_Praja_Parishad#Jammu_agitation_\(1949%E2%80%931953\)) by [Jammu Praja Parishad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jammu_Praja_Parishad), a Hindu nationalist party which was active in the Jammu region, over the ruling National Conference's policies. The government swiftly suppressed it by arresting as many as 294 members of the Praja Parishad including Prem Nath Dogra, its president. Though Sheikh's land reforms were said to have benefited the people of rural areas, Praja Parishad opposed the 'Landed Estates Abolition Act', saying it was against the Indian Constitutional rights, for implementing land acquisition without compensation. Praja Parishad also called for the full integration with the rest of India, directly clashing with the demands of National Conference for complete autonomy of the state. On 15 January 1952, students staged a demonstration against the hoisting of the state flag alongside the Indian Union flag. They were penalised, giving rise to a big procession on 8 February. The military was called out and a 72-hour curfew imposed. [N. Gopalaswami Ayyangar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/N._Gopalaswami_Ayyangar), the Indian Central Cabinet minister in charge of Kashmir affairs, came down to broker peace, which was resented by Sheikh Abdullah.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGupta,_Jammu_and_Kashmir2012195,_196-221][^cite_note-\:10-217]

In order to break the constitutional deadlock, Nehru invited the National Conference to send a delegation to Delhi. The '1952 Delhi Agreement' was formulated to settle the extent of applicability of the Indian Constitution to the Jammu and Kashmir and the relation between the State and Centre. It was reached between Nehru and Abdullah on 24 July 1952. Following this, the Constituent Assembly abolished the monarchy in Kashmir, and adopted an elected Head of State (*Sadr-i Riyasat*). However, the Assembly was reluctant to implement the remaining measures agreed to in the Delhi Agreement.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGupta,_Jammu_and_Kashmir2012197–203-222][^cite_note-223]

In 1952, [Sheikh Abdullah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sheikh_Abdullah) drifted from his previous position of endorsing accession to India to insisting on the self-determination of Kashmiris.[^cite_note-224]

The Praja Parishad undertook a civil disobedience campaign for a third time in November 1952, which again led to repression by the state government. The Parishad accused Abdullah of communalism (sectarianism), favouring the Muslim interests in the state and sacrificing the interests of the others. The Jana Sangh joined hands with the [Hindu Mahasabha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindu_Mahasabha) and [Ram Rajya Parishad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ram_Rajya_Parishad) to launch a parallel agitation in Delhi. In May 1953, [Shyama Prasad Mukherjee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syama_Prasad_Mukherjee), a prominent Indian leader of the time and the founder of Hindu nationalist party [Bharatiya Jana Sangh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bharatiya_Jana_Sangh) (later evolved as [BJP](149330.md)), made a bid to enter Jammu and Kashmir after denying to take a permit, citing his rights as an Indian citizen to visit any part of the country. Abdullah prohibited his entry and promptly arrested him when he attempted. An estimated 10,000 activists were imprisoned in Jammu, Punjab and Delhi, including Members of Parliament. Unfortunately, Mukherjee died in detention on 23 June 1953, leading to an uproar in whole India and precipitating a crisis that went out of control.[^cite_note-225][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGupta,_Jammu_and_Kashmir2012197–203-222]

Observers state that Abdullah became upset, as he felt, his "absolute power" was being compromised in India.[^cite_note-226]

Meanwhile, Nehru's pledge of a referendum to people of Kashmir did not come into action. Sheikh Abdullah advocated complete independence and had allegedly joined hands with US to conspire against India.[^cite_note-227]

On 8 August 1953, Sheikh Abdullah was dismissed as prime minister by the *[Sadr-i-Riyasat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sadr-i-Riyasat)* [Karan Singh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karan_Singh) on the charge that he had lost the confidence of his [cabinet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cabinet_\(government\)). He was denied the opportunity to prove his majority on the floor of the house. He was also jailed in 1953 while Sheikh's dissident deputy, [Bakshi Ghulam Mohammad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bakshi_Ghulam_Mohammad) was appointed as the new [Prime Minister of the state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Chief_Ministers_of_Jammu_and_Kashmir).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbdullah,_Atish-e-Chinar1985593–607-228]

### Period of integration and rise of Kashmiri separatism (1954–1974)

> From all the information I have, 95 per cent of Kashmir Muslims do not wish to be or remain Indian citizens. I doubt therefore the wisdom of trying to keep people by force where they do not wish to stay. This cannot but have serious long-term political consequences, though immediately it may suit policy and please public opinion.

— [Jayaprakash Narayan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jayaprakash_Narayan)'s letter to Nehru, May 1, 1956.[^cite_note-229]

Bakshi Mohammad implemented all the measures of the '1952 Delhi Agreement'.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGupta,_Jammu_and_Kashmir2012209–212-230] In May 1954, as a subsequent to the Delhi agreement,[^cite_note-\:22-231] [The Constitution (Application to Jammu and Kashmir) Order, 1954](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Constitution_\(Application_to_Jammu_and_Kashmir\)_Order,_1954), is issued by the President of India under Article 370, with the concurrence of the Government of the State of Jammu and Kashmir. In that order, the [Article 35A](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Article_35A_of_Constitution_of_India) is added to the Constitution of India to empower the Jammu and Kashmir state's legislature to define "permanent residents" of the state and provide special rights and privileges to those permanent residents.[^cite_note-\:02-232]

On 15 February 1954, under the leadership of Bakshi Mohammad, the [Constituent Assembly of Jammu and Kashmir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constituent_Assembly_of_Jammu_and_Kashmir) ratified the [state's accession to India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Instrument_of_Accession_\(Jammu_and_Kashmir\)).[^cite_note-233][^cite_note-234] On 17 November 1956, the [Constitution of Jammu and Kashmir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Jammu_and_Kashmir) was adopted by the Assembly and it came into full effect on 26 January 1957.[^cite_note-\:6-235] On 24 January 1957, the UN passed a [resolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Security_Council_Resolution_122) stating that the decisions of the Constituent Assembly would not constitute a final disposition of the State, which needs to be carried out by a free and impartial plebiscite.[^cite_note-236]

Meanwhile, in Pakistan administered [Azad Jammu and Kashmir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azad_Jammu_and_Kashmir), the [1955 Poonch uprising](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1955_Poonch_uprising) begins in February 1955 against the governments dismissal of Sardar Ibrahim Khan. The rebellion would only be quashed in 1956.[^cite_note-\:0-237]

After the overthrow of Sheikh Abdullah, his lieutenant Mirza Afzal Beg formed the [Plebiscite Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plebiscite_Front) on 9 August 1955 to fight for the plebiscite demand and the unconditional release of Sheikh Abdullah. The activities of the Plebiscite Front eventually led to the institution of the infamous [Kashmir Conspiracy Case](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kashmir_Conspiracy_Case) in 1958 and two other cases. On 8 August 1958, Abdullah was arrested on the charges of these cases.[^cite_note-238]

India's Home Minister, Pandit [Govind Ballabh Pant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Govind_Ballabh_Pant), during his visit to Srinagar in 1956, declared that the State of Jammu and Kashmir was an integral part of India and there could be no question of a plebiscite to determine its status afresh, hinting that India would resist plebiscite efforts from then on.[^cite_note-239]

After the mass unrest due to [missing of holy relic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theft_of_the_Holy_Relic_from_the_Hazratbal_Shrine) from the [Hazratbal Shrine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hazratbal_Shrine) on 27 December 1963, the State Government dropped all charges in the [Kashmir Conspiracy Case](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kashmir_Conspiracy_Case) as a diplomatic decision, on 8 April 1964. Sheikh Abdullah was released and returned to Srinagar where he was accorded a great welcome by the people of the valley. After his release he was reconciled with Nehru. Nehru requested Sheikh Abdullah to act as a bridge between India and Pakistan and make President [Ayub Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayub_Khan_\(Field_Marshal\)) of Pakistan agree to come to New Delhi for the talks for a final solution of the Kashmir problem. President Ayub Khan also sent telegrams to Nehru and Sheikh Abdullah with the message that as Pakistan too was a party to the Kashmir dispute any resolution of the conflict without its participation would not be acceptable to Pakistan. Sheikh Abdullah went to Pakistan in the spring of 1964. President Ayub Khan held extensive talks with him to explore various avenues for solving the Kashmir problem and agreed to come to [Delhi](37756.md) in mid June for talks with Nehru as suggested by him. Even the date of his proposed visit was fixed and communicated to New Delhi. However, while Abdullah was still in Pakistan, news came of the sudden death of Nehru on 27 May 1964. The peace initiative died with Nehru.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbdullah,_Atish-e-Chinar1985752–786-240]

After Nehru's death in 1964, Abdullah was interned from 1965 to 1968 and exiled from Kashmir in 1971 for 18 months. The [Plebiscite Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plebiscite_Front) was also banned. This was allegedly done to prevent him and the Plebiscite Front which was supported by him, from taking part in elections in Kashmir.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbdullah,_Atish-e-Chinar1985817–825-241]

On 21 November 1964, the [Articles 356 and 357](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President's_rule) of the Indian Constitution were extended to the state, by virtue of which the Central Government can assume the government of the State and exercise its legislative powers. On 24 November 1964, the Jammu and Kashmir Assembly passed a constitutional amendment changing the elected post of *Sadr-i-Riyasat* to a centrally-nominated post of "Governor" and renaming "Prime Minister" to "Chief Minister", which is regarded as the "end of the road" for the Article 370, and the Constitutional autonomy guaranteed by it.[^cite_note-\:6-235] On 3 January 1965, prior to [1967 Assembly elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1967_Jammu_and_Kashmir_Legislative_Assembly_election), the Jammu and Kashmir National Conference dissolved itself and merged into the [Indian National Congress](149333.md), as a marked centralising strategy.[^cite_note-242]

After [Indo-Pakistani War of 1965](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Pakistani_War_of_1965), Kashmiri nationalists [Amanullah Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amanullah_Khan_\(JKLF\)) and [Maqbool Bhat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maqbool_Bhat), along with [Hashim Qureshi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hashim_Qureshi?action=edit\&redlink=1), in 1966, formed another [Plebiscite Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azad_Kashmir_Plebiscite_Front) in Azad Kashmir with an armed wing called the [National Liberation Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azad_Kashmir_Plebiscite_Front#National_Liberation_Front) (NLF), with the objective of freeing Kashmir from Indian occupation and then liberating the whole of Jammu and Kashmir. Later in 1976, Maqbool Bhat is arrested on his return to the Valley. Amanullah Khan moved to England and there NLF was renamed [Jammu and Kashmir Liberation Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jammu_and_Kashmir_Liberation_Front) (JKLF).

Shortly after 1965 war, Kashmiri Pandit activist and writer, [Prem Nath Bazaz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prem_Nath_Bazaz) wrote that the overwhelming majority of Kashmir's Muslims were unfriendly to India and wanted to get rid of the political setup, but did not want to use violence for this purpose. He added : "It would take another quarter century of repression and generation turnover for the pacifist approach to yield decisively as armed struggle, qualifying Kashmiris as 'reluctant secessionists'."[^cite_note-Bose_p.84-85-243]

In 1966 the Indian opposition leader [Jayaprakash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jayaprakash_Narayan) wrote to Indian Prime Minister Indira Gandhi that India rules Kashmir by force.[^cite_note-Bose_p.84-85-243]

In 1974, the State Subject law was officially abolished in Gilgit Baltistan, which allowed any Pakistani to settle and buy land.[^cite_note-DawnGilgitBaltistan-244]

### Revival of National Conference (1975–1983)

In 1971, the declaration of Bangladesh's independence was proclaimed on 26 March by Sheikh Mujibur Rahman, and subsequently the [Bangladesh Liberation War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangladesh_Liberation_War) broke out in erstwhile [East Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Pakistan) between Pakistan and Bangladesh which was later joined by India, and subsequently [war broke out](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Pakistani_War_of_1971) on the western border of India between India and Pakistan, both of which culminated in the creation of [Bangladesh](3454.md).

It is said that, Sheikh Abdullah, watching the alarming turn of events in the subcontinent, realized that for the survival of the region, there was an urgent need to stop pursuing confrontational politics and promoting solution of issues by a process of reconciliation and dialogue. Critics of Sheikh hold the view that he gave up the cherished goal of plebiscite for gaining Chief Minister's chair. He started talks with the then Prime Minister [Indira Gandhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indira_Gandhi) for normalizing the situation in the region and came to an accord with her, called [1975 Indira-Sheikh accord](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1974_Indira-Sheikh_accord), by giving up the demand for a plebiscite in lieu of the people being given the right to self-rule by a democratically elected Government (as envisaged under [article 370 of the Constitution of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Article_370_of_the_Constitution_of_India)), rather than the "puppet government" which is said to have ruled the state until then.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbdullah,_Atish-e-Chinar1985827–838-245] Sheikh Abdullah revived the [National Conference](512306.md), and Mirza Afzal Beg's [Plebiscite Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/All_Jammu_and_Kashmir_Plebiscite_Front) was dissolved in the [NC](512306.md). Sheikh assumed the position of [Chief Minister of Jammu and Kashmir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Chief_Ministers_of_Jammu_and_Kashmir) again after 11 years. Later in 1977, the Central Government and the ruling Congress Party withdrew its support so that the State Assembly had to be dissolved and mid term elections called. Sheikh's party [National Conference](512306.md) won a majority (47 out of 74 seats) in the subsequent elections, on the pledge to restore Jammu and Kashmir's autonomy, and Sheikh Abdullah was re-elected as Chief Minister. The [1977 Assembly election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1977_Jammu_and_Kashmir_Legislative_Assembly_election) is regarded as the first "free and fair" election in the Jammu and Kashmir state.[^cite_note-\:6-235][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbdullah,_Atish-e-Chinar1985860–882-246][^cite_note-247]

He remained as Chief Minister of Jammu and Kashmir until his death in 1982. Later his eldest son [Farooq Abdullah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Farooq_Abdullah) succeeded him as the [Chief Minister of the state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Chief_Ministers_of_Jammu_and_Kashmir).

During the [1983 Assembly elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1983_Jammu_and_Kashmir_Legislative_Assembly_election), Indira Gandhi campaigned aggressively, raising the bogey of a 'Muslim invasion' in the Jammu region because of the Resettlement Bill, passed by the then NC government, which gave Kashmiris who left for Pakistan between 1947 and 1954 the right to return, reclaim their properties and resettle. On the other hand, Farooq Abdullah allied with the [Mirwaiz Maulvi Mohammed Farooq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mirwaiz_Maulvi_Farooq) for the elections and charged that the state's autonomy had been eroded by successive Congress Party governments. The strategies yielded dividends and the Congress won 26 seats, while the NC secured 46. Barring an odd constituency, all victories of the Congress were in the Jammu and Ladakh regions, while NC swept the Kashmir Valley. This election is said to have cemented the political polarization on religious lines in the Jammu and Kashmir state.[^cite_note-248][^cite_note-Weaver-249]

After the results of the 1983 election, the Hindu nationalists in the state were demanding stricter central government control over the state whereas Kashmir's Muslims wanted to preserve the state's autonomy. Islamic fundamentalist groups clamoured for a plebiscite. Maulvi Farooq challenged the contention that there was no longer a dispute on Kashmir. He said that the people's movement for plebiscite would not die even though India thought it did when Sheikh Abdullah died.[^cite_note-Weaver-249]

In 1983, learned men of Kashmiri politics testified that Kashmiris had always wanted to be independent. But the more serious-minded among them also realised that this is not possible, considering Kashmir's size and borders.[^cite_note-Weaver-249]

According to historian [Mridu Rai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mridu_Rai), for three decades Delhi's handpicked politicians in Kashmir had supported the State's accession to India in return for generous disbursements from Delhi. Rai states that the state elections were conducted in Jammu and Kashmir, but except for the [1977](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1977_Jammu_and_Kashmir_Legislative_Assembly_election) and [1983](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1983_Jammu_and_Kashmir_Legislative_Assembly_election) elections no [state election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elections_in_Jammu_and_Kashmir#Legislative_assembly_elections) was fair.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMridu_Rai,_Hindu_Rulers,_Muslim_Subjects2004289-250]

Kashmiri Pandit activist Prem Nath Bazaz wrote that if free elections were held, the majority of seats would be won by those not friendly to India.[^cite_note-Bose_p.84-85-243]

### Rise of the separatist movement and Islamism (1984–1986)

See also: [1986 Anantnag Riots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1986_Anantnag_Riots)

Increasing anti-Indian protests took place in Kashmir in the 1980s. The Soviet-Afghan jihad and the Islamic Revolution in Iran were becoming sources of inspiration for large numbers of Kashmiri Muslim youth. The state authorities responded with increasing use of brute force to simple economic demands. Both the pro-Independence Jammu and Kashmir Liberation Front (JKLF) and the pro-Pakistan Islamist groups including JIJK mobilised the fast growing anti-Indian sentiments among the Kashmiri population.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESikand2002745-251] 1984 saw a pronounced rise in terrorist violence in Kashmir. When [Kashmir Liberation Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jammu_Kashmir_Liberation_Front) militant [Maqbool Bhat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maqbool_Bhat) was executed in February 1984, strikes and protests by Kashmiri nationalists broke out in the region. Large numbers of Kashmiri youth participated in widespread anti India demonstrations, which faced heavy handed reprisals by Indian state forces.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESikand2002705–751_(746)-252][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPraveen_Swami;_India,_Pakistan_and_the_Secret_Jihad2007157-253] Critics of the then Chief Minister, Farooq Abdullah, charged that Abdullah was losing control. His visit to Pakistan administered Kashmir became an embarrassment, where according to [Hashim Qureshi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hashim_Qureshi?action=edit\&redlink=1), he shared a platform with Kashmir Liberation Front. Though Abdullah asserted that he went on behalf of [Indira Gandhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indira_Gandhi) and his father, so that sentiments there could "be known first hand", few people believed him. There were also allegations that he had allowed [Khalistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khalistan_movement) terrorist groups to train in Jammu province, although those allegations were never proved. On 2 July 1984, [Ghulam Mohammad Shah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghulam_Mohammad_Shah), who had support from Indira Gandhi, replaced his brother-in-law Farooq Abdullah and became the chief minister of Jammu and Kashmir, after Abdullah was dismissed, in what was termed as a political "coup".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPraveen_Swami;_India,_Pakistan_and_the_Secret_Jihad2007157-253]

In 1986 some members of the JKLF crossed over to Pakistan to receive arms training but the Jamaat Islami Jammu Kashmir, which saw Kashmiri nationalism as contradicting Islamic universalism and its own desire for merging with Pakistan, did not support the JKLF movement. As late as that year, Jamaat member Syed Ali Shah Geelani, who later became a supporter of Kashmir's armed revolt, urged that the solution for the Kashmir issue be arrived at through peaceful and democratic means.[^cite_note-254] To achieve its goal of self-determination for the people of Jammu and Kashmir the Jamaat e Islami's stated position was that the Kashmir issues be resolved through constitutional means and dialogue.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESikand2002746-255]

Shah's administration, which did not have the people's mandate, turned to Islamists and opponents of India, notably the [Molvi Iftikhar Hussain Ansari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Molvi_Iftikhar_Hussain_Ansari?action=edit\&redlink=1), [Mohammad Shafi Qureshi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad_Shafi_Qureshi) and Mohinuddin Salati, to gain some legitimacy through religious sentiments. This gave political space to Islamists who previously lost overwhelmingly, allegedly due to massive rigging,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESikand2002744–745-256] in the 1983 state elections.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPraveen_Swami;_India,_Pakistan_and_the_Secret_Jihad2007157-253] In 1986, Shah decided to construct a mosque within the premises of an ancient Hindu temple inside the New Civil Secretariat area in Jammu to be made available to the Muslim employees for 'Namaz'. People of Jammu took to streets to protest against this decision, which led to a Hindu-Muslim clash.[^cite_note-257] On his return to Kashmir valley in February 1986, Gul Shah retaliated and incited the Kashmiri Muslims by saying *Islam khatrey mein hey* (trans. Islam is in danger). As a result, communal violence gripped the region, in which Hindus were targeted, especially the Kashmiri pandits, who later in the year 1990, [fled the valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exodus_of_Kashmiri_Hindus) in large numbers. During the [Anantnag riot in February 1986](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1986_Anantnag_riots), many houses and other properties belonging to Hindus were looted, burnt or damaged.[^cite_note-Tikoo-258][^cite_note-259]

Shah called in the army to curb the violence on the Hindus, but it had little effect. His government was dismissed on 12 March 1986, by the then Governor [Jagmohan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jagmohan) following communal riots in south Kashmir. This led Jagmohan to rule the state directly.

Jagmohan is said to have failed to distinguish between the secular forms and Islamist expressions of Kashmiri identity, and hence saw that identity as a threat. This failure was exploited by the Islamists of the valley, who defied the 'Hindu nationalist' policies implemented during Jagmohan's tenure, and thereby gained momentum. The political fight was hence being portrayed as a conflict between "Hindu" New Delhi (Central Government), and its efforts to impose its will in the state, and "Muslim" Kashmir, represented by political Islamists and clerics.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPraveen_Swami;_India,_Pakistan_and_the_Secret_Jihad2007158-260] Jagmohan's pro-Hindu bias in the administration led to an increase in the appeal of the Muslim United Front.[^cite_note-Schofield2000-261]

### 1987 state elections

Main article: [1987 Jammu and Kashmir Legislative Assembly election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1987_Jammu_and_Kashmir_Legislative_Assembly_election)

An alliance of Islamic parties organized into [Muslim United Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_United_Front) (MUF) to contest the [1987 state elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1987_Jammu_and_Kashmir_Legislative_Assembly_election).[^cite_note-262] Culturally, the growing emphasis on secularism led to a backlash with Islamic parties becoming more popular.[^cite_note-263] MUF's election manifesto stressed the need to solve all outstanding issues according to the [Simla agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simla_agreement), work for Islamic unity and against political interference from the centre. Their slogan was wanting the law of the Quran in the Assembly.[^cite_note-264]

There was highest recorded participation in this election. 80% of the people in the Valley voted. MUF received victory in only 4 of the contested 43 electoral constituencies despite its high vote share of 31 per cent (this means that its official vote in the Valley was larger than one-third). The elections were widespreadly believed to have been rigged by the ruling party [National Conference](512306.md), allied with the [Indian National Congress](149333.md).[^cite_note-j\&khistory-265][^cite_note-266][^cite_note-267][^cite_note-\:3-268] In the absence of rigging, commentators believe that the MUF could have won fifteen to twenty seats, a contention admitted by the National Conference leader [Farooq Abdullah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Farooq_Abdullah).[^cite_note-\:3-268][^cite_note-Brass1994-269] Scholar [Sumantra Bose](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sumantra_Bose), on the other hand. opines that the MUF would have won most of the constituencies in the Kashmir Valley.[^cite_note-270]

[BBC News](1139893.md) reported that Khem Lata Wukhloo, who was a leader of the Congress party at the time, admitted the widespread rigging in Kashmir. He stated: I remember that there was a massive rigging in 1987 elections. The losing candidates were declared winners. It shook the ordinary people's faith in the elections and the democratic process.[^cite_note-271]

Meanwhile, in Pakistan administered Gilgit Baltistan, the state sponsored [1988 Gilgit Massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1988_Gilgit_Massacre) led by [Osama bin Laden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Osama_bin_Laden) and other extremist groups take place.[^cite_note-Undaunted-272]

### 1989 popular insurgency and militancy

Main article: [Insurgency in Jammu and Kashmir](1915235.md)

> In the years since 1990, the Kashmiri Muslims and the Indian government have conspired to abolish the complexities of Kashmiri civilization. The world it inhabited has vanished: the state government and the political class, the rule of law, almost all the Hindu inhabitants of the valley, alcohol, cinemas, cricket matches, picnics by moonlight in the saffron fields, schools, universities, an independent press, tourists and banks. In this reduction of civilian reality, the sights of Kashmir are redefined: not the lakes and Mogul gardens, or the storied triumphs of Kashmiri agriculture, handicrafts and cookery, but two entities that confront each other without intermediary: the mosque and the army camp.

— British journalist [James Buchan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Buchan)[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGuha,_India_after_Gandhi2008654-273]

In 1989, a widespread popular and armed insurgency[^cite_note-274][^cite_note-275] started in Kashmir. After the 1987 state legislative assembly election, some of the results were disputed. This resulted in the formation of militant wings and marked the beginning of the [Mujahadeen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mujahadeen) insurgency, which continues to this day.[^cite_note-\:16-276][^cite_note-\:8-277] India contends that the insurgency was largely started by Afghan mujahadeen who entered the Kashmir valley following the end of the [Soviet–Afghan War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet%E2%80%93Afghan_War).[^cite_note-\:8-277] [Yasin Malik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yasin_Malik), a leader of one faction of the [Jammu Kashmir Liberation Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jammu_Kashmir_Liberation_Front_\(Yasin_Malik\)) (JKLF), was one of the Kashmiris to organise militancy in Kashmir, along with [Ashfaq Majeed Wani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashfaq_Majeed_Wani), Javaid Ahmad Mir, and Abdul Hamid Sheikh. Since 1995, Malik has renounced the use of violence and calls for strictly peaceful methods to resolve the dispute. Malik developed differences with one of the senior leaders, Farooq Siddiqui (alias Farooq Papa), for shunning demands for an independent Kashmir and trying to cut a deal with the Indian Prime Minister. This resulted in a split in which [Bitta Karate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Farooq_Ahmed_Dar), Salim Nanhaji, and other senior comrades joined Farooq Papa.[^cite_note-278][^cite_note-279] Pakistan claims these insurgents are Jammu and Kashmir citizens, and are rising up against the Indian army as part of an independence movement. [Amnesty International](18947898.md) has accused security forces in Indian-controlled Kashmir of exploiting an [Armed Forces (Special Powers) Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armed_Forces_\(Special_Powers\)_Act) that enables them to "hold prisoners without trial". The group argues that the law, which allows security forces to detain individuals for up to two years without presenting charges violates prisoners' human rights.[^cite_note-280][^cite_note-BBC-281] In 2011, the state humans right commission said it had evidence that 2,156 bodies had been buried in 40 graves over the last 20 years.[^cite_note-BBC-281] The authorities deny such accusations. The security forces say the unidentified dead are militants who may have originally come from outside India. They also say that many of the missing people have crossed into Pakistani-administered Kashmir to engage in militancy.[^cite_note-BBC-281] However, according to the state human rights commission, among the identified bodies 574 were those of "disappeared locals", and according to Amnesty International's annual human rights report (2012) it was sufficient for "belying the security forces' claim that they were militants".[^cite_note-AI-282]

India claims these insurgents are [Islamic terrorist](566821.md) groups from Pakistani-administered Kashmir and Afghanistan, fighting to make Jammu and Kashmir a part of Pakistan.[^cite_note-BBC-281][^cite_note-283] Indian analysts[^cite_note-\:17-284] and the JKLF have accused Pakistan of training and backing terrorists.[^cite_note-285][^cite_note-286] India states that the terrorists have killed many citizens in Kashmir and committed human rights violations whilst denying that their own armed forces are responsible for human rights abuses. On a visit to Pakistan in 2006, former Chief Minister of Kashmir [Omar Abdullah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Omar_Abdullah) remarked that foreign militants were engaged in reckless killings and mayhem in the name of religion.[^cite_note-287] Indian authorities said in 2008 and 2021 that militancy was on the decline.[^cite_note-\:16-276][^cite_note-online.wsj.com-288]

The Pakistani government denies it supports terrorists, only saying it has supported "freedom fighters" in the past.[^cite_note-289] In October 2008, President [Asif Ali Zardari](993655.md) of Pakistan called the Kashmir separatists "terrorists" in an interview with *[The Wall Street Journal](173070.md)*.[^cite_note-Dawn:_Zardari-290] These comments sparked outrage amongst many Kashmiris, some of whom defied a curfew imposed by the Indian army to burn him in effigy.[^cite_note-291]

In 2008, pro-separatist leader [Mirwaiz Umar Farooq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mirwaiz_Umar_Farooq) told the *[Washington Post](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Post)* that there has been a "purely indigenous, purely Kashmiri"[^cite_note-washingtonpost.com-292] peaceful protest movement alongside the insurgency in Indian-administered Kashmir since 1989. The movement was created for the same reason as the insurgency and began after the disputed election of 1987. According to the United Nations, the Kashmiris have grievances with the Indian government, specifically the Indian military, which has committed human rights violations.[^cite_note-washingtonpost.com-292][^cite_note-online.wsj.com-288][^cite_note-unhchr.ch-293]

In 1994, the NGO [International Commission of Jurists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Commission_of_Jurists) sent a fact finding mission to Kashmir. The ICJ mission concluded that the right of self-determination to which the peoples of Jammu and Kashmir became entitled as part of the process of partition had neither been exercised nor abandoned, and thus remained exercisable.[^cite_note-294] It further stated that as the people of Kashmir had a right of self-determination, it followed that their insurgency was legitimate. It, however, did not follow that Pakistan had a right to provide support for the militants.[^cite_note-295]

### 1989–1990 exodus of Kashmir Pandits

Main article: [Exodus of Kashmiri Hindus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exodus_of_Kashmiri_Hindus)

|  | This section **may be [too long](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:Section#Section_size_policies) or excessively detailed.** Please consider summarising the material. *(August 2019)*_&#x20;([Learn how and when to remove this message](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:Maintenance_template_removal))_ |
| --- | --- |

Due to rising insurgency and Islamic militancy in the Kashmir Valley, [Kashmiri Pandits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kashmiri_Pandit) were forced to flee the valley.[^cite_note-296] They were targeted by militant groups such as the [Jammu Kashmir Liberation Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jammu_Kashmir_Liberation_Front), [Lashkar-e-Taiba](649718.md), and [Jaish-e-Mohammed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jaish-e-Mohammed). On 4 January 1990, [Srinagar](294148.md) based newspaper *Aftab* released a message, threatening all Hindus to leave Kashmir immediately, sourcing it to the militant organization [Hizbul Mujahideen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hizbul_Mujahideen).[^cite_note-297][^cite_note-298] In the preceding months, around 300 Hindu men and women, Kashmiri Pandits, had been slaughtered and women raped. Mosque released statement in loud speaker asked Hindus to leave Kashmir without their women. On 19 January 1990, Kashmiri Pandits fled from Kashmiri due to atrocities such as killing and gang rape.[^cite_note-299][^cite_note-Rediff-300]

On 21 January 1990, two days after [Jagmohan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jagmohan) took over as [governor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governor_\(India\)) of Jammu and Kashmir, the [Gawkadal massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gawkadal_massacre) took place in Srinagar when the Indian paramilitary troops of the [Central Reserve Police Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Reserve_Police_Force) opened fire on a group of Kashmiri protesters in what has been described by some authors as "the worst massacre in Kashmiri history" (along with the [Bijbehara Massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bijbehara_Massacre) in 1993).[^cite_note-VS-301] At least 50 people were killed,[^cite_note-302] with some reports of the deaths reaching as high as 280.[^cite_note-303] In the aftermath of the massacre, more demonstrations followed, and in January 1990, Indian paramilitary forces are believed to have killed around 300 protesters.[^cite_note-304] As a [Human Rights Watch](46530.md) stated in a report from May 1991, "In the weeks that followed \[the Gawakadal massacre] as security forces fired on crowds of marchers and as militants intensified their attacks against the police and those suspected of aiding them, Kashmir's civil war began in earnest."[^cite_note-305]

The mass exodus began on 1 March 1990, when hundreds of thousands of Kashmiri Pandits left the state; of the approximately 300,000[^cite_note-306][^cite_note-307][^cite_note-308] to 600,000[^cite_note-309][^cite_note-310] Hindus living in the Kashmir Valley in 1990, only 2,000–3,000 lived there in 2016.[^cite_note-311]

### 2000s Al-Qaeda involvement

Main article: [Al-Qaeda](1921.md)

See also: [Allegations of support system in Pakistan for Osama bin Laden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allegations_of_support_system_in_Pakistan_for_Osama_bin_Laden)

In a 'Letter to American People' written by [Osama bin Laden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Osama_bin_Laden) in 2002, he stated that one of the reasons he was fighting America was because of its support for India on the Kashmir issue.[^cite_note-312] While on a trip to Delhi in 2002, US Secretary of Defense [Donald Rumsfeld](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donald_Rumsfeld) suggested that [Al-Qaeda](1921.md) was active in Kashmir, though he did not have any hard evidence.[^cite_note-313][^cite_note-SMH-314] An investigation by a *[Christian Science Monitor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Christian_Science_Monitor)* reporter in 2002 claimed to have unearthed evidence that Al-Qaeda and its affiliates were prospering in [Pakistani-administered Kashmir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistani-administered_Kashmir) with tacit approval of Pakistan's [Inter-Services Intelligence](2159021.md) agency (ISI).[^cite_note-315] In 2002, a team comprising [Special Air Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_Air_Service) and [Delta Force](328809.md) personnel was sent into [Indian-administered Kashmir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian-administered_Kashmir) to hunt for Osama bin Laden after reports that he was being sheltered by the Kashmiri militant group [Harkat-ul-Mujahideen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harkat-ul-Mujahideen).[^cite_note-316] US officials believed that Al-Qaeda was helping organise a campaign of terror in Kashmir to provoke conflict between India and Pakistan. Their strategy was to force Pakistan to move its troops to the border with India, thereby relieving pressure on Al-Qaeda elements hiding in northwestern Pakistan. US intelligence analysts say Al-Qaeda and [Taliban](30635.md) operatives in Pakistani-administered Kashmir are helping terrorists trained in Afghanistan to infiltrate Indian-administered Kashmir.[^cite_note-317] [Fazlur Rehman Khalil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fazlur_Rehman_Khalil), the leader of the Harkat-ul-Mujahideen, signed al-Qaeda's 1998 declaration of holy war, which called on Muslims to attack all Americans and their allies.[^cite_note-CFRKashmir-318] In 2006 Al-Qaeda claim they have established a wing in Kashmir, which worried the Indian government.[^cite_note-319] Indian Army Lieutenant General H.S. Panag, GOC-in-C Northern Command, told reporters that the army has ruled out the presence of Al-Qaeda in Indian-administered Jammu and Kashmir. He said that there no evidence to verify media reports of an Al-Qaeda presence in the state. He ruled out Al-Qaeda ties with the militant groups in Kashmir including [Lashkar-e-Taiba](649718.md) and [Jaish-e-Mohammed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jaish-e-Mohammed). However, he stated that they had information about Al Qaeda's strong ties with Lashkar-e-Taiba and Jaish-e-Mohammed operations in Pakistan.[^cite_note-320] While on a visit to Pakistan in January 2010, US Defense secretary [Robert Gates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Gates) stated that Al-Qaeda was seeking to destabilise the region and planning to provoke a nuclear war between India and Pakistan.[^cite_note-321]

In June 2011, a US [Drone strike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drone_attacks_in_Pakistan) killed [Ilyas Kashmiri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ilyas_Kashmiri_\(militant\)), chief of [Harkat-ul-Jihad al-Islami](5592225.md), a Kashmiri militant group associated with Al-Qaeda.[^cite_note-322][^cite_note-323] Kashmiri was described by [Bruce Riedel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bruce_Riedel) as a 'prominent' Al-Qaeda member,[^cite_note-324] while others described him as the head of military operations for Al-Qaeda.[^cite_note-325] [Waziristan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waziristan) had by then become the new battlefield for Kashmiri militants fighting [NATO](21133.md) in support of Al-Qaeda.[^cite_note-The_News\:Ilyas-326] Ilyas Kashmiri was charged by the US in a plot against *[Jyllands-Posten](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jyllands-Posten)*, the Danish newspaper at the center of the [Jyllands-Posten Muhammad cartoons controversy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jyllands-Posten_Muhammad_cartoons_controversy).[^cite_note-327] In April 2012, [Farman Ali Shinwari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Farman_Ali_Shinwari) a former member of Kashmiri separatist groups [Harkat-ul-Mujahideen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harkat-ul-Mujahideen) and [Harkat-ul-Jihad al-Islami](5592225.md), was appointed chief of al-Qaeda in Pakistan.[^cite_note-rediff-328]

### 2008–present

|  | This section needs to be **updated**. The reason given is: **Needs more recent information, "recent" as in last 10 years**. Please help update this article to reflect recent events or newly available information. *(April 2025)* |
| --- | --- |



[Kashmir Solidarity Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kashmir_Solidarity_Day) on every 5 February is observed in Pakistan. This banner was hung in [Islamabad](51153.md).

In March 2008, two separate incidents were reported in Indian-administered Kashmir- a blast near the civil secretariat and high court, and a gun battle between security forces and militants which left five dead. The gunfight began when security forces raided a house on the outskirts of the capital city of [Srinagar](294148.md) housing militants.[^cite_note-\:12-329] The [Indian Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Army) has been carrying out cordon-and-search operations against militants in Indian-administered Kashmir since the violence broke out in 1989.[^cite_note-\:12-329]

Massive demonstrations followed a May 2008 decision of the [state government of Jammu and Kashmir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_of_Jammu_and_Kashmir) to [transfer 100 acres (0.40 km<sup>2</sup>) of land](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amarnath_land_transfer_controversy) to a trust which runs the Hindu [Amarnath shrine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amarnath_temple) in the Muslim-majority Kashmir valley.[^cite_note-330] This land was to be used to build a shelter to house Hindu pilgrims temporarily during their annual pilgrimage to the [Amarnath temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amarnath_temple).[^cite_note-\:13-331] Indian security forces including the army responded quickly to keep order. More than 40 unarmed protesters were killed.[^cite_note-332][^cite_note-333] The largest protests saw more than a half million people waving Pakistani flags and crying for freedom at a rally on 18 August, according to *[Time magazine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Time_magazine)*.[^cite_note-\:13-331][^cite_note-334] The situation drew international reactions from separatist leaders and the United Nations.[^cite_note-Dawn\:Mirwaiz-335][^cite_note-unhchr.ch-293] Following the unrest in 2008, secessionist movements got a boost.[^cite_note-336][^cite_note-\:13-331] Such demonstrations have been aloof of the fact that the India government very regularly undertakes activities to uplift the Muslim community and donates lands and other properties to the systemized [Waqf Boards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Waqf_Council).[^cite_note-337][^cite_note-338] Despite the protests, [state elections in November–December 2008](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_Jammu_and_Kashmir_Legislative_Assembly_election) in Indian administered Kashmir saw a high voter turnout of more than 60% of the total registered electors.[^cite_note-339][^cite_note-340]









Visual of voters during parliamentary general elections being conducted in Indian-Jammu and Kashmir in 2004 and 2009. A boycott graffiti seen in Old City, Srinagar.

In 2009, protests started over the alleged [rape and murder of two young women](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2009_Shopian_rape_and_murder_case) in [Shopian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shopian) in South Kashmir.[^cite_note-341] The next summer again saw large-scale protests with the immediate trigger being a [fake encounter staged by the military in Machil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_Machil_encounter), [Kupwara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kupwara_district).[^cite_note-342] This [2010 Kashmir unrest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_Kashmir_unrest) saw separatist sentiments, pro-independence slogans, protestors who defied curfews, attacked security forces with stones and burnt police vehicles and government buildings.[^cite_note-343][^cite_note-nyt20100911-344][^cite_note-nyt20100804-345] Security forces in Jammu and Kashmir fired live ammunition on the protesters, resulting in 112 deaths. The protests subsided after the Indian government announced a package of measures aimed at defusing the tensions in September 2010.[^cite_note-Reuters20100930-346]

Revelations made on 24 September 2013 by the former Indian army chief [General V. K. Singh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/V._K._Singh) claim that the state politicians of Jammu and Kashmir are funded by the army secret service to keep the general public calm and that this activity has been going on since Partition.[^cite_note-347][^cite_note-348][^cite_note-349]

In October 2014, [Indian and Pakistani troops traded LOC gunfire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014%E2%80%932015_India%E2%80%93Pakistan_border_skirmishes) – the small-arms and mortar exchanges – which Indian officials called the worst violation of a 2003 ceasefire – left soldiers and civilians dead. Thousands of people fled their homes on both sides after the violence erupted on 5 October.[^cite_note-350]

The [2014 Jammu and Kashmir Legislative Assembly election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Jammu_and_Kashmir_Legislative_Assembly_election) was held from 25 November – 20 December. Despite repeated boycott calls by separatist [Hurriyat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/All_Parties_Hurriyat_Conference) leaders,[^cite_note-351] elections recorded highest voters turnout in last 25 years, that is more than 65% which is more than usual voting percentage in other states of [India](14533.md).[^cite_note-Deccan_Chronicle-352][^cite_note-in.com-353][^cite_note-ndtv.com-354] Phase wise voting percentage is as follows (table):

| Voting phases |  | Date | Seats | Turnout |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
|  |  | 25 November | 15 | 71% |
|  | 2 December | 18 | 71% |  |
|  | 9 December | 16 | 59% |  |
|  | 14 December | 18 | 49% |  |
|  | 20 December | 20 | 76% |  |
|  | **Total** | **87** | **65%** |  |
| Sources:[^cite_note-355][^cite_note-TO-356][^cite_note-357][^cite_note-358][^cite_note-359] |  |  |  |  |

The [European Parliament](9581.md) welcomed the smooth conduct of the State Legislative Elections in the Jammu and Kashmir despite boycott calls.[^cite_note-prnewswire.com-360] The [EU](9317.md) in its message said, "The high voter turnout figure proves that democracy is firmly rooted in India. The EU would like to congratulate India and its democratic system for conduct of fair elections, unmarred by violence, in the state of Jammu and Kashmir".[^cite_note-prnewswire.com-360][^cite_note-dailyexcelsior.com-361]

On 8 July 2016, a militant leader [Burhan Wani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burhan_Muzaffar_Wani) was cornered by the security forces and killed. Following his death, [protests and demonstrations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016%E2%80%932017_Kashmir_unrest) led to an "amplified instability" in the Kashmir valley. Curfews were imposed in all 10 districts of Kashmir, over 100 civilians died and over 17,000 were injured in clashes with the police.[^cite_note-362][^cite_note-363][^cite_note-364] More than 600 have pellet injuries to the face. To prevent volatile rumours, cellphone and internet services were blocked, and newspapers were also restricted in many parts of the region.[^cite_note-365]

An attack by four militants on an Indian Army base on 18 September 2016, also known as the [2016 Uri attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Uri_attack), resulted in the death of 19 soldiers as well as the militants themselves.[^cite_note-366] Response took various forms, including the postponement of the [19th SAARC summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/19th_SAARC_summit),[^cite_note-367] asking the Russian government to call off a joint military exercise with Pakistan,[^cite_note-368][^cite_note-369] and the 'Indian Motion Picture Producers Association' decision to suspend work with Pakistan.[^cite_note-370] On the Pakistani side, military alertness was raised and some [Pakistan International Airlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan_International_Airlines) flights suspended. The Pakistani government denied any role and raised the issue of human rights violations by Indian security forces.[^cite_note-371]



Indian police in Kashmir valley confronting violent protesters in December 2018

In the deadliest incident since 2016, [Jaish-e-Mohammed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jaish-e-Mohammed) (JEM) carried and claimed responsibility for [a suicide bomb attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Pulwama_attack) on a military convoy in Pulwama that killed over 40 Indian soldiers on 14 February 2019.[^cite_note-372] In [retaliation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Balakot_airstrike) 12 Indian fighter jets dropped bombs on a "terrorist camp" in Pakistan-controlled territory of Kashmir, allegedly killing around 350 members in terrorist camps. As India trespassed Pakistan's air space, the incidents escalated the tension between India and Pakistan starting the [2019 India–Pakistan border standoff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_India%E2%80%93Pakistan_standoff) and skirmishes.[^cite_note-373][^cite_note-374][^cite_note-375][^cite_note-376][^cite_note-377] On 22 March 2019, a peace offer was fixed, ending the hostilities, and with both countries agreeing to fight terrorism.[^cite_note-378][^cite_note-379]

In August 2019, India [revoked the special status of Jammu and Kashmir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revocation_of_the_special_status_of_Jammu_and_Kashmir) through Parliament, [abolishing Article 370](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Article_370_of_the_Constitution_of_India) and rendering the state [Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Jammu_and_Kashmir) infructuous. Further both houses of the Indian parliament passed a [bill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jammu_and_Kashmir_Reorganisation_Act_\(2019\)) to reorganise the state into two union territories.[^cite_note-380][^cite_note-381] This was followed by a strict preventive [pre-emptive state lockdown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019%E2%80%932021_Jammu_and_Kashmir_lockdown), which lasted until 5 February 2021 (A preventative lockdown was put in place after the death of Syed Ali Shah Geelani for 2 to 5 days).[^cite_note-382][^cite_note-383][^cite_note-\:3522-384] LOC border [clashes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020%E2%80%9321_India%E2%80%93Pakistan_border_skirmishes) between November 2020 and February 2021 resulted in 24 deaths of both military personnel and civilians.[^cite_note-385][^cite_note-386]

In April 2025, three suspected militants and an Indian army officer were killed in separate encounters in the Indian-controlled region. On 9 April, the [Indian army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Army) engaged militants in [Kishtwar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kishtwar) district, resulting in the death of one militant, with two more killed in subsequent firefights. On 11 April, in the [Akhnoor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akhnoor) area, Indian soldiers intercepted militants attempting to infiltrate from [Pakistan](23235.md), leading to the death of an army officer.[^cite_note-387] On 22 April, Islamist militants [attacked a tourist spot in Pahalgam](79778862.md), causing the Indian government to attack alleged terrorist camps in Pakistan, as part of a [four-day intense conflict](79896622.md) the following May[^cite_note-us_dia_report-388] that ended in ceasefire.[^cite_note-BBC_IN_PK_live-389]

## National stances

| Administered by | Area | Population | % [Muslim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim) | % [Hindu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindu) | % [Buddhist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist) | % Other |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| India | Kashmir Valley | \~4 million | 95% | 4% | – | – |
| Jammu | \~3 million | 30% | 66% | – | 4% |  |
| Ladakh | \~0.25 million | 46% | – | 50% | 3% |  |
| Pakistan | Gilgit-Baltistan | \~1 million | 99% | – | – | – |
| Azad Kashmir | \~2.6 million | 100% | – | – | – |  |
| China | Aksai Chin | – | – | – | – | – |
| Shaksgam Valley | – | – | – | – | – |  |
| Statistics from the [BBC](19344654.md) report "In Depth"[^cite_note-390] 525,000 refugees from Indian-administered Kashmir migrated to Pakistan and Azad Kashmir in 1947–48.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKorbel,_Danger_in_Kashmir1966153-391] 226,000 refugees from Pakistani-administered Kashmir migrated to India and Jammu and Kashmir in 1947 and 1948.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKorbel,_Danger_in_Kashmir1966153-391] A minimum of 506,000 people in the Indian-administered Kashmir Valley are [internally displaced](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internally_displaced) due to militancy in Kashmir, about half of whom are [Hindu pandits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kashmiri_Pandit)[^cite_note-392] Muslims form the majority in the [Poonch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poonch_district_\(J\&K\)), [Rajouri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rajouri_district), [Kishtwar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kishtwar_District), and [Doda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doda_District) districts of the Jammu region. [Shia](26961.md) Muslims make up the majority in the [Kargil district](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kargil_district) in the Ladakh region. India does not accept the [two-nation theory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Two_Nation_Theory) and considers that Kashmir, despite being a Muslim-majority region, is in many ways an "integral part" of [secular India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secularism_in_India).[^cite_note-Hardgrave,_Robert._-India:_The_Dilemmas_of_Diversity-393] |  |  |  |  |  |  |

### Indian view



[Maharaja Hari Singh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maharaja_Hari_Singh) signed the [Instrument of Accession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Instrument_of_Accession_\(Jammu_and_Kashmir\)) in October 1947 under which he acceded the [State of Jammu and Kashmir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kashmir_and_Jammu_\(princely_state\)) to the Union of India.



Map showing [disputed territories of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disputed_territories_of_India)

India has officially stated that it believes Kashmir to be an integral part of India, though the then Prime Minister of India, [Manmohan Singh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manmohan_Singh), stated after the [2010 Kashmir Unrest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_Kashmir_Unrest) that his government was willing to grant autonomy to the region within the purview of the Indian constitution if there was consensus among political parties on this issue.[^cite_note-394][^cite_note-395] The Indian viewpoint is succinctly summarised by the Indian [Ministry of External affairs](10302410.md).[^cite_note-meaindweb-396][^cite_note-meaindpdf-397] —

- India holds that the [Instrument of Accession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Instrument_of_Accession_\(Jammu_and_Kashmir\)) of the State of Jammu and Kashmir to the [Union of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dominion_of_India), signed by Maharaja Hari Singh (erstwhile ruler of the State) on 25 October 1947

  [^cite_note-398]

  [^cite_note-Chief_Justice_JSAnand-399]

  &#x20;and executed on 27 October 1947

  [^cite_note-Chief_Justice_JSAnand-399]

  &#x20;between the ruler of Kashmir and the Governor General of India was a legal act and completely valid in terms of the Government of India Act (1935), Indian Independence Act (1947) as well as under international law and as such was total and irrevocable.

  [^cite_note-meaindpdf-397]

- The [Constituent assembly of Jammu and Kashmir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constituent_assembly_of_Jammu_and_Kashmir) had unanimously ratified the Maharaja's Instrument of Accession to India and adopted a constitution for the state that called for a perpetual merger of Jammu and Kashmir with the Union of India. India claims that the constituent assembly was a representative one, and that its views were those of the Kashmiri people at the time.

  [\[note 8\]](#cite_note-Sayyid_Mir_Qasim-104)

  [^cite_note-400]

- [United Nations Security Council Resolution 1172](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Security_Council_Resolution_1172) tacitly accepts India's stand regarding all outstanding issues between India and Pakistan and urges the need to resolve the dispute through mutual dialogue without the need for a plebiscite in the framework of UN Charter.

  [^cite_note-401]

  [^cite_note-402]

- [United Nations Security Council Resolution 47](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Security_Council_Resolution_47) cannot be implemented since Pakistan failed to withdraw its forces from Kashmir, which was the first step in implementing the resolution.

  [^cite_note-telegraph2001-403]

  &#x20;India is also of the view that Resolution 47 is obsolete, since the geography and demographics of the region have permanently altered since it adoption.

  [^cite_note-404]

  &#x20;The resolution was passed by [United Nations Security Council](31956.md) under [Chapter VI of the United Nations Charter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chapter_VI_of_the_United_Nations_Charter) and as such is non-binding with no mandatory enforceability, as opposed to resolutions passed under Chapter VII.

  [^cite_note-pakun.org-405]

  [^cite_note-dawn.com-406]

- India does not accept the [two-nation theory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Two_Nation_Theory) that forms the basis of Pakistan's claims and considers that Kashmir, despite being a Muslim-majority region, is in many ways an "integral part" of [secular India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secularism_in_India).

  [^cite_note-Hardgrave,_Robert._-India:_The_Dilemmas_of_Diversity-393]

- The state of Jammu and Kashmir was provided with significant autonomy under [Article 370](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Article_370) of the [Constitution of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_India).

  [^cite_note-407]

- All differences between India and Pakistan, including Kashmir, need to be settled through bilateral negotiations as agreed to by the two countries under the Simla Agreement signed on 2 July 1972.

  [^cite_note-408]

Additional Indian viewpoints regarding the broader debate over the Kashmir conflict include:

- In a diverse country like India, disaffection and discontent are not uncommon. Indian democracy has the necessary resilience to accommodate genuine grievances within the framework of India's sovereignty, unity, and integrity. The Government of India has expressed its willingness to accommodate the legitimate political demands of the people of the state of Kashmir.

  [^cite_note-meaindweb-396]

- Insurgency and terrorism in Kashmir is deliberately fuelled by Pakistan to create instability in the region.

  [^cite_note-409]

  &#x20;The Government of India has repeatedly accused Pakistan of waging a [proxy war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proxy_war) in Kashmir by providing weapons and financial assistance to [terrorist groups](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan_and_state_terrorism) in the region.

  [^cite_note-410]

  [^cite_note-411]

  [^cite_note-412]

  [^cite_note-Dawn:_Congress-413]

- Pakistan is trying to raise anti-India sentiment among the people of Kashmir by spreading false propaganda against India.

  [^cite_note-propaganda-414]

  &#x20;According to the state government of Jammu and Kashmir, Pakistani radio and television channels deliberately spread "hate and venom" against India to alter Kashmiri opinion.

  [^cite_note-415]

- India has asked the United Nations not to leave unchallenged or unaddressed the claims of moral, political, and diplomatic support for terrorism, which were clearly in contravention of [United Nations Security Council Resolution 1373](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Security_Council_Resolution_1373). This is a [Chapter VII](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chapter_VII_of_the_United_Nations_Charter) resolution that makes it mandatory for member states to not provide active or passive support to terrorist organisations.

  [^cite_note-416]

  [^cite_note-417]

  &#x20;Specifically, it has pointed out that the Pakistani government continues to support various terrorist organisations, such as [Jaish-e-Mohammad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jaish-e-Mohammed) and [Lashkar-e-Taiba](649718.md), in direct violation of this resolution.

  [^cite_note-418]

- India points out reports by human rights organisations condemning Pakistan for the lack of civic liberties in Pakistani-administered Kashmir.

  [^cite_note-propaganda-414]

  [^cite_note-419]

  &#x20;According to India, most regions of Pakistani Kashmir, especially Northern Areas, continue to suffer from lack of political recognition, economic development, and basic fundamental rights.

  [^cite_note-420]

- [Karan Singh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karan_Singh), the son of the last ruler of the princely state of Kashmir and Jammu, has said that the Instrument of Accession signed by his father was the same as signed by other states. He opined that Kashmir was therefore a part of India, and that its special status granted by [Article 370](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Article_370) of the Indian Constitution stemmed from the fact that it had its own constitution.

  [^cite_note-421]

According to a poll in an Indian newspaper Indians were keener to keep control of Kashmir than Pakistanis. 67% of urban Indians want New Delhi to be in full control of Kashmir.[^cite_note-reuters.com-422]

[Michigan State University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michigan_State_University) scholar Baljit Singh, interviewing Indian foreign policy experts in 1965, found that 77 percent of them favoured discussions with Pakistan on all outstanding problems including the Kashmir dispute. However, only 17 percent were supportive of holding a plebiscite in Kashmir. The remaining 60 percent were pessimistic of a solution due to a distrust of Pakistan or a perception of threats to India's internal institutions. They contended that India's secularism was far from stable and the possibility of Kashmir separating from India or joining Pakistan would endanger Hindu–Muslim relations in India.[^cite_note-423]

In 2008, the death toll from the last 20 years was estimated by Indian authorities to be over 47,000.[^cite_note-hindustantimes.com-424]

In 2017 India's Union Home Minister, [Rajnath Singh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rajnath_Singh), demanded that Pakistan desist from demanding a plebiscite in Jammu and Kashmir, saying: "If at all a referendum is required, it is needed in Pakistan, where people should be asked whether they want to continue in Pakistan or are demanding the country's merger with India".[^cite_note-425]

### Pakistani view

Pakistan maintains that Kashmir is the "jugular vein of Pakistan"[^cite_note-dawndec2006-426] and a currently disputed territory whose final status must be determined by the people of Kashmir.[^cite_note-427][^cite_note-428] Pakistan's claims to the disputed region are based on the rejection of Indian claims to Kashmir, namely the Instrument of Accession. Pakistan insists that the Maharaja was not a popular leader, and was regarded as a tyrant by most Kashmiris. Pakistan maintains that the Maharaja used brute force to suppress the population.[^cite_note-429]

Pakistan claims that Indian forces were in Kashmir before the Instrument of Accession was signed with India, and that therefore Indian troops were in Kashmir in violation of the [Standstill Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standstill_Agreement), which was designed to maintain the status quo in Kashmir (although India was not signatory to the Agreement, which was signed between Pakistan and the Hindu ruler of Jammu and Kashmir).[^cite_note-430][^cite_note-431]

From 1990 to 1999, some organisations reported that the [Indian Armed Forces](7171338.md), its paramilitary groups, and counter-insurgent militias were responsible for the deaths of 4,501 Kashmiri civilians. During the same period, there were records of 4,242 women between the ages of 7–70 being raped.[^cite_note-432][^cite_note-433] Similar allegations were also made by some human rights organisations.[^cite_note-434]

In short, Pakistan holds that:

- The popular Kashmiri insurgency demonstrates that the Kashmiri people no longer wish to remain within India. Pakistan suggests that this means that Kashmir either wants to be with Pakistan or independent.

  [^cite_note-435]

- According to the [two-nation theory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Two-nation_theory), one of the principles that is cited for the partition that created India and Pakistan, Kashmir should have been with Pakistan, because it has a Muslim majority.

- India has shown disregard for the resolutions of the UN Security Council and the United Nations Commission in India and Pakistan by failing to hold a plebiscite to determine the future allegiance of the state.

  [^cite_note-436]
