- The reason for India's disregard of the resolutions of the UN Security Council was given by India's Defense Minister, Kirshnan Menon, who said: "Kashmir would vote to join Pakistan and no Indian Government responsible for agreeing to plebiscite would survive."

  [^cite_note-Kashmir_Old_Headache-437]

- Pakistan was of the view that the Maharaja of Kashmir had no right to call in the Indian Army, because it held that the Maharaja of Kashmir was not a hereditary ruler and was merely a British appointee, after the British defeated Ranjit Singh who ruled the area before the British conquest.

  [^cite_note-438]

- Pakistan has noted the widespread use of [extrajudicial killings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extrajudicial_killing) in Indian-administered Kashmir carried out by Indian security forces while claiming they were caught up in encounters with militants. These encounters are commonplace in Indian-administered Kashmir. The encounters go largely uninvestigated by the authorities, and the perpetrators are spared criminal prosecution.

  [^cite_note-439]

  [^cite_note-440]

- Pakistan disputes claims by India with reference to the Simla Agreement that UN resolutions on Kashmir have lost their relevance. It argues that legally and politically, UN Resolutions cannot be superseded without the UN Security Council adopting a resolution to that effect. It also maintains the Simla Agreement emphasised exploring a peaceful bilateral outcome, without excluding the role of UN and other negotiations. This is based on its interpretation of Article 1(i) stating "the principles and purposes of the Charter of the United Nations shall govern the relations between the two countries".

  [^cite_note-441]

Human rights organisations have strongly condemned Indian troops for widespread rape and murder of innocent civilians while accusing these civilians of being militants.[^cite_note-442][^cite_note-443][^cite_note-444]

- The Chenab formula was a compromise proposed in the 1960s, in which the Kashmir valley and other Muslim-dominated areas north of the [Chenab river](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chenab_river) would go to Pakistan, and Jammu and other Hindu-dominated regions would go to India.

  [^cite_note-445]

A poll by an Indian newspaper shows 48% of Pakistanis want Islamabad "to take full control" of Kashmir, while 47% of Pakistanis support Kashmiri independence.[^cite_note-reuters.com-422]

Former Pakistani President General [Pervez Musharraf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pervez_Musharraf) on 16 October 2014 said that Pakistan needs to incite those fighting in Kashmir,[^cite_note-tribune.com.pk-446][^cite_note-timesofindia.indiatimes.com-447] "We have source (in Kashmir) besides the (Pakistan) army…People in Kashmir are fighting against (India). We just need to incite them," Musharraf told a TV channel.[^cite_note-tribune.com.pk-446][^cite_note-timesofindia.indiatimes.com-447]

In 2015 Pakistan's outgoing National Security Advisor [Sartaj Aziz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sartaj_Aziz) said that Pakistan wished to have third party mediation on Kashmir, but it was unlikely to happen unless by international pressure.[^cite_note-Pak_wishes_to_have_3rd_party_mediation_on_Kashmir:_Aziz-448] "Under [Shimla Accord](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simla_Agreement) it was decided that India and Pakistan would resolve their disputes bilaterally," Aziz said. "Such bilateral talks have not yielded any results for the last 40 years. So then what is the solution?"[^cite_note-Pak_wishes_to_have_3rd_party_mediation_on_Kashmir:_Aziz-448]

### Chinese view

See also: [Origins of the Sino-Indian border dispute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Origins_of_the_Sino-Indian_border_dispute)

China has generally supported Pakistan against India on Kashmir.[^cite_note-\:17-284][^cite_note-449] China has also stated that [Aksai Chin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aksai_Chin) is an integral part of China and does not recognise its inclusion in the Kashmir region. It also disputes the region's boundary with [Tibet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tibet_Autonomous_Region) at various locations.

- China did not accept the boundaries of the princely state of Kashmir and Jammu, north of Aksai Chin and the [Karakoram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karakoram) as proposed by the British.

  [^cite_note-britannica1-450]

- China settled its border disputes with Pakistan under the 1963 [Sino-Pakistan Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sino-Pakistan_Agreement) on the [Trans-Karakoram Tract](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trans_Karakoram_Tract) with the provision that the settlement was subject to the final solution of the Kashmir dispute.

  [^cite_note-451]

  [^cite_note-Time-5]

### Kashmiri views

- Scholar Andrew Whitehead states that Kashmiris view Kashmir as having been ruled by their own in 1586. Since then, they believe, it has been ruled in succession by the [Mughals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mughal_Empire), [Afghans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Durrani_Empire), [Sikhs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sikh_Empire), [Dogras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dogra_dynasty) and, lately, the Indian government. Whitehead states that this is only partly true: the Mughals lavished much affection and resources on Kashmir, the Dogras made Srinagar their capital next only to their native Jammu city, and through much of the post-independence India, Kashmiri Muslims headed the state government. According to Whitehead, Kashmiris bear an 'acute sense of grievance' that they were not in control of their own fate for centuries.

  [^cite_note-452]

- [A. G. Noorani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A._G._Noorani), a constitutional expert, says the people of Kashmir are 'very much' a party to the dispute.

  [^cite_note-453]

- According to an opinion poll conducted by [Centre for the Study of Developing Societies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centre_for_the_Study_of_Developing_Societies) in 2007, 87% of people in mainly Muslim Srinagar want independence, whereas 95% of the people in the mainly Hindu Jammu city think the state should be part of India.

  [^cite_note-\:5-454]

  &#x20;The Kashmir Valley is the only region of the former princely state where the majority of the population is unhappy with its current status. The Hindus of Jammu and Buddhists of Ladakh are content under Indian administration. Muslims of Azad Kashmir and Northern Areas are content under Pakistani administration. Kashmir Valley's Muslims want to change their national status to independence.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchaffer,_The_Limits_of_Influence20093-455]

- Scholar A.G. Noorani testifies that Kashmiris want a plebiscite to achieve freedom.

  [^cite_note-456]

  &#x20;Zutshi states the people of Poonch and Gilgit may have had a chance to determine their future but the Kashmiri was lost in the process.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZutshi,_Languages_of_Belonging2004309-457]

- Since the 1947 accession of Kashmir to India was *provisional* and *conditional* on the wishes of the people,

  [^cite_note-tandfonline.com-458]

  &#x20;the Kashmiris' right to determine their future was recognised.

  [^cite_note-459]

  &#x20;Noorani notes that state elections do not satisfy this requirement.

  [^cite_note-460]

- Kashmiris assert that except for 1977 and 1983 elections, no state election has been fair.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMridu_Rai,_Hindu_Rulers,_Muslim_Subjects2004289-250]

  &#x20;According to scholar [Sumantra Bose](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sumantra_Bose), India was determined to stop fair elections since that would have meant that elections would be won by those unfriendly to India.

  [^cite_note-Bose_p.84-85-243]

- The Kashmiri people have still not been able to exercise the right to self-determination and this was the conclusion of the International Commission of Jurists in 1994.

  [^cite_note-Hassan_Abbas-461]

- Ayesha Parvez writes in *[The Hindu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Hindu)* that high voter turnout in Kashmir cannot be interpreted as a sign of acceptance of Indian rule. Voters vote due to varying factors such as development, effective local governance and economy.

  [^cite_note-462]

- The Hurriyat parties do not want to participate in elections under the framework of the Indian Constitution. Elections held by India are seen as a diversion from the main issue of self-determination.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchofield,_Kashmir_in_Conflict2003168-463]

- Kashmiri opponents to Indian rule maintain that India has stationed 600,000 Indian troops in what is the highest ratio of troops to civilian density in the world.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchofield,_Kashmir_in_Conflict2003168-463]

- Kashmiri scholars say that India's military occupation inflicts violence and humiliation on Kashmiris. Indian forces are responsible for human rights abuses and terror against the local population and have killed tens of thousands of civilians. India's state forces have used rape as a cultural weapon of war against Kashmiris and rape has extraordinarily high incidence in Kashmir as compared to other conflict zones of the world.

  [^cite_note-464]

  &#x20;Militants are also guilty of crimes but their crimes cannot be compared with the scale of abuses by Indian forces for which justice is yet to be delivered.

- Kashmiri scholars say that India's reneging on promise of plebiscite, violations of constitutional provisions of Kashmir's autonomy and subversion of the democratic process led to the rebellion of 1989–1990.

  [^cite_note-465]

- According to historian Mridu Rai, the majority of Kashmiri Muslims believe they are scarcely better off under Indian rule than the 101 years of Dogra rule.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMridu_Rai,_Hindu_Rulers,_Muslim_Subjects2004288-466]

- [Markandey Katju](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Markandey_Katju), an ethnic Kashmiri and former Justice of the [Supreme Court of India](395852.md), maintains that the secession of Kashmir would cause its economy to suffer, due to the fact that Kashmir's handicraft industry is dependent on buyers in other parts of India; Katju holds that the ultimate solution to the Kashmir conflict is the [reunification of what is now Pakistan with India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_reunification) under a secular government.

  [^cite_note-Katju2020-467]

  [^cite_note-468]

- According to lawyer and human rights activist [K. Balagopal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K._Balagopal), Kashmiris have a distinct sense of identity and this identity is certainly not irreligious, as Islam is very much a part of the identity that Kashmiris feel strongly for. He opined that if only non-religious identities deserve support, then no national self-determination movement can be supported, because there is no national identity  – at least in the Third World –  devoid of the religious dimension. Balagopal says that if India and Pakistan cannot guarantee existence and peaceful development of independent Kashmir then Kashmiris may well choose Pakistan because of religious affinity and social and economic links. But if both can guarantee existence and peaceful development then most Kashmiris would prefer independent Kashmir.

  [^cite_note-\:4-469]

  [^cite_note-470]

### Water dispute

In 1948, [Eugene Black](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eugene_R._Black_Sr.), then executive director of the World Bank, offered his services to solve the tension over water control. In the early days of independence, the fact that India was able to shut off the Central Bari Doab Canals at the time of the sowing season, causing significant damage to Pakistan's crops. Nevertheless, military and political clashes over Kashmir in the early years of independence appear to have been more about ideology and sovereignty rather than over the sharing of water resources. However, the minister of Pakistan has stated the opposite.[^cite_note-471]

The [Indus Waters Treaty](3603967.md) was signed by both countries in September 1960, giving exclusive rights over the three western rivers of the Indus river system (Jhelum, Chenab and Indus) to Pakistan, and over the three eastern rivers (Sutlej, Ravi and Beas) to India, as long as this does not reduce or delay the supply to Pakistan. India therefore maintains that they are not willing to break the established regulations and they see no more problems with this issue.

## Efforts to end the dispute

There has been little meaningful dialogue to end the long-standing conflict. As of 2024, India holds the territorially advantageous position. Proposed solutions include independence for Kashmir, formal partition between India and Pakistan, and greater autonomy for the states of [Azad Kashmir](2745.md) and [Jammu and Kashmir](61449812.md).[^cite_note-Kurr_2015_p.-472]

### Settlement formulas

#### Rajaji-Abdullah formula

The Rajaji-Abdullah formula, named after [C. Rajagopalachari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C._Rajagopalachari) and [Sheikh Abdullah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sheikh_Abdullah), also simply called the Rajaji formula, was in Abdullah's words "an honourable solution which would not give a sense of victory either to India or Pakistan and at the same time would ensure a place of honour to the people of Kashmir".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGuha,_Opening_a_Window_in_Kashmir200487-473] The discussions in 1964 covered numerous options, a plebiscite, status quo, further division etc., however seemed to point towards a [condominium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Condominium_\(international_law\)), a shared government, a [confederation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confederation) and a [United Nations trust territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_trust_territories).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGuha,_Opening_a_Window_in_Kashmir200487-473][^cite_note-\:15-474] Abdullah would act as the mediator between India and Pakistan.[^cite_note-\:15-474] While the exact nature of the proposed settlement was never made public, Ayub Khan would go on to write in his autobiography that the proposal was "absurd".[^cite_note-475]

#### Chenab formula

In 2005, General Musharraf, as well as other Pakistani leaders, sought to resolve the Kashmir issue through the Chenab formula road map. Borrowing a term used by Owen Dixon, Musharraf's Chenab Formula assigns Ladakh to India, Gilgit-Baltistan (G-B) to Pakistan, proposes a plebiscite in the Kashmir Valley and splits Jammu into two-halves.[^cite_note-476] On 5 December 2006, Pakistani President [Pervez Musharraf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pervez_Musharraf) told an Indian TV channel that Pakistan would give up its claim on Kashmir if India accepted some of his peace proposals, including a phased withdrawal of troops, self-governance for locals, no changes in the borders of Kashmir, and a joint supervision mechanism involving India, Pakistan, and Kashmir.[^cite_note-477] Musharraf stated that he was ready to give up the United Nations' resolutions regarding Kashmir.[^cite_note-478]

Later, the government of Pakistan said that this was Musharraf's personal opinion.[^cite_note-479] However Satinder Lambah, India's special envoy to Pakistan, says that while talks reduced due to the Mumbai attacks, the formula was not disowned.[^cite_note-480]

### Contemporary views on UN resolutions

Many neutral parties to the dispute have noted that the UN resolution on Kashmir is no longer relevant.[^cite_note-481] The European Union holds the view that the plebiscite is not in Kashmiris' interest.[^cite_note-482] The report notes that the UN conditions for such a plebiscite have not been, and can no longer be, met by Pakistan.[^cite_note-483] The [Hurriyat Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hurriyat_Conference) observed in 2003 that a "plebiscite \[is] no longer an option".[^cite_note-484]

A 2002 [Market and Opinion Research International (MORI)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ipsos_MORI) survey, on the basis of 850 interviews, found that within Indian-administered Kashmir, 61% of respondents said they felt they would be better off as Indian citizens, with 33% saying that they did not know, and the remaining 6% favouring Pakistani citizenship. However, this support for India was mainly in the Ladakh and Jammu regions, not the Kashmir Valley, where only 9% of the respondents said that they would be better off with India.[^cite_note-485]&#x20;

According to a 2007 poll conducted by the [Centre for the Study of Developing Societies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centre_for_the_Study_of_Developing_Societies) in New Delhi involving around 400 people, 87% of respondents in the Kashmir Valley prefer independence over union with India or Pakistan.[^cite_note-486] A survey by [Chatham House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chatham_House), on the basis of 3,774 face-to face interviews in Indian administered [Jammu and Kashmir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jammu_and_Kashmir_\(state\)) and Pakistan administered [Azad Kashmir](2745.md),[^cite_note-487] found that support for independence stood at 43% and 44% respectively.[^cite_note-488]

## Pakistan's relation with militants

In a 2001 commentary entitled *Pakistan's Role in the Kashmir Insurgency* in *[Jane's Intelligence Review](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jane's_Intelligence_Review)*, the author noted that "the nature of the Kashmir conflict has been transformed from what was originally a secular, locally based struggle (conducted via the [Jammu Kashmir Liberation Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jammu_Kashmir_Liberation_Front) – JKLF) to one that is now largely carried out by foreign militants and rationalized in pan-Islamic religious terms." The majority of militant organisations are composed of foreign mercenaries, mostly from the [Pakistani Punjab](24751.md).[^cite_note-489]

In 2010, with the support of its intelligence agencies, Pakistan again 'boosted' Kashmir militants, and recruitment of [mujahideen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mujahideen) in the Pakistani state of Punjab has increased.[^cite_note-490]

[^cite_note-bbc05142010-491] In 2011, the [FBI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FBI) revealed that Pakistan's spy agency [ISI](2159021.md) paid millions of dollars into a United States–based non-governmental organisation to influence politicians and opinion-makers on the Kashmir issue and arrested [Syed Ghulam Nabi Fai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syed_Ghulam_Nabi_Fai).[^cite_note-492]

Some political analysts say that the Pakistan state policy shift and mellowing of its aggressive stance may have to do with its total failure in the [Kargil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kargil_War) and the subsequent [9/11](5058690.md) attacks. These events put pressure on Pakistan to alter its position on terrorism.[^cite_note-493]

[Former President of Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Presidents_of_Pakistan) and the ex-chief of the Pakistan military [Pervez Musharraf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pervez_Musharraf), stated in an interview in London, that the Pakistani government indeed helped to form underground militant groups and "turned a blind eye" towards their existence because they wanted India to discuss Kashmir.[^cite_note-Speigel-494]

According to former [Indian Prime-minister](24452.md) [Manmohan Singh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manmohan_Singh), one of the main reasons behind the conflict was Pakistan's "terror-induced coercion". He further stated at a Joint Press Conference with United States President [Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama) in New Delhi that India is not afraid of resolving all the issues with Pakistan including that of Kashmir "*but it is our request that you cannot simultaneously be talking and at the same time the terror machine is as active as ever before. Once Pakistan moves away from this terror-induced coercion, we will be very happy to engage productively with Pakistan to resolve all outstanding issues.*"[^cite_note-495]

In 2009, the [President of Pakistan](419460.md) [Asif Zardari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asif_Zardari) asserted at a conference in Islamabad that Pakistan had indeed created Islamic militant groups as a strategic tool for use in its geostrategic agenda and "to attack Indian forces in Jammu and Kashmir".[^cite_note-496] Former president of Pakistan and the ex-chief of the Pakistan military Pervez Musharraf also stated in an interview that Pakistani government helped to form underground militant groups to fight against Indian troops in [Jammu and Kashmir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jammu_and_Kashmir_\(state\)) and "turned a blind eye" towards their existence because they wanted India to discuss Kashmir.[^cite_note-Speigel-494] The British Government have formally accepted that there is a clear connection between Pakistan's [Inter-Services Intelligence](2159021.md) (ISI) and three major militant outfits operating in [Jammu and Kashmir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kashmir), [Lashkar-e-Tayiba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lashkar-e-Tayiba), [Jaish-e-Mohammed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jaish-e-Mohammed) and [Harkat-ul-Mujahideen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harkat-ul-Mujahideen).[^cite_note-497][^cite_note-498] The militants are provided with "weapons, training, advice and planning assistance" in Punjab and Kashmir by the ISI which is "*coordinating the shipment of arms from the Pakistani side of Kashmir to the Indian side, where Muslim insurgents are waging a protracted war*".[^cite_note-499]

Throughout the 1990s, the ISI maintained its relationship with extremist networks and militants that it had established during the Afghan war to utilise in its campaign against Indian forces in Kashmir. [Joint Intelligence/North](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joint_Intelligence/North) (JIN) has been accused of conducting operations in Jammu and Kashmir and also [Afghanistan](737.md).[^cite_note-Camp-500] The Joint Signal Intelligence Bureau (JSIB) provide communications support to groups in Kashmir.[^cite_note-Camp-500] According to [Daniel Benjamin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daniel_Benjamin) and [Steven Simon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steven_Simon), both former members of the [National Security Council](44026.md), the ISI acted as a "kind of terrorist conveyor belt" radicalising young men in the [Madrassas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madrassa) of Pakistan and delivering them to training camps affiliated with or run by Al-Qaeda and from there moving them into [Jammu and Kashmir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kashmir) to launch attacks.[^cite_note-Caldwell-501]

Reportedly, about [Rs.](588111.md) 24 million are paid out per month by the ISI to fund its activities in Jammu and Kashmir.[^cite_note-FAS-502] [Pro-Pakistani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pro-Pakistani) groups were reportedly favoured over other militant groups.[^cite_note-FAS-502] Creation of six militant groups in Kashmir, which included [Lashkar-e-Taiba](649718.md) (LeT), was aided by the ISI.[^cite_note-Salon-503][^cite_note-\:14-504] According to [American Intelligence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US_intelligence) officials, ISI is still providing protection and help to LeT.[^cite_note-\:14-504] The [Pakistan Army](1965925.md) and ISI also LeT volunteers to surreptitiously penetrate from [Pakistan Administrated Kashmir](2745.md) to Jammu and Kashmir.[^cite_note-tellis2010-505]

In the past, Indian authorities have alleged several times that Pakistan has been involved in training and arming underground militant groups to fight Indian forces in Kashmir.[^cite_note-Musharraf-506]

## Human rights abuses

Main article: [Human rights abuses in Kashmir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_rights_abuses_in_Kashmir)

In the *Freedom in the World* 2025 report, Indian-administered Kashmir was categorized as "partly-free",[^cite_note-507] while Pakistani-administered Kashmir was categorized as "not free".[^cite_note-508] India and Pakistan themselves were both categorized as "partly free".[^cite_note-509][^cite_note-510]

### Indian administered Kashmir

Main article: [Human rights abuses in Jammu and Kashmir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_rights_abuses_in_Jammu_and_Kashmir)

Further information: [Rape during the Kashmir conflict](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rape_during_the_Kashmir_conflict)



A soldier guards the roadside checkpoint outside Srinagar International Airport in January 2009.

Crimes by state forces are done inside Kashmir Valley which is the location of the present conflict.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKazi,_Rape,_Impunity_and_Justice_in_Kashmir201414-511]

The 2010 Chatham House opinion poll of the people of Indian administered Jammu and Kashmir found that overall concern, in the entire state, over human rights abuses was 43%.[^cite_note-\:1-512] In the surveyed districts of the Muslim majority Kashmir Valley, where the desire for Independence is strongest,[^cite_note-\:2-513] there was a high rate of concern over human rights abuses. (88% in Baramulla, 87% in Srinagar, 73% in Anantnag and 55% in Badgam).[^cite_note-\:1-512] However, in the Hindu and Buddhist majority areas of the state, where pro-India sentiment is extremely strong,[^cite_note-\:2-513] concern over human rights abuses was low (only 3% in Jammu expressed concerns over human rights abuses).[^cite_note-\:1-512]

&#x20;Several international agencies and the UN have reported human rights violations in [Indian-administered Kashmir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian-administered_Kashmir). In a 2008 press release the OHCHR spokesmen stated "The Office of the High Commissioner for Human Rights is concerned about the recent violent protests in Indian-administered Kashmir that have reportedly led to civilian casualties as well as restrictions to the right to freedom of assembly and expression."[^cite_note-unhchr.ch-293] A 1996 [Human Rights Watch](46530.md) report accuses the Indian military and Indian-government backed paramilitaries of "committ\[ing] serious and widespread human rights violations in Kashmir."[^cite_note-514] Jammu and Kashmir Coalition of Civil Society labels the happenings in Kashmir as war crimes and genocide and have issued a statement that those responsible should be tried in court of law.[^cite_note-Kashmir_Observer-515][^cite_note-Vltchek-516] Some of the massacres by security forces include [Gawakadal massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gawakadal_massacre), [Zakoora and Tengpora massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zakoora_and_Tengpora_massacre) and [Handwara massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Handwara_massacre). Another such alleged massacre occurred on 6 January 1993 in the town of [Sopore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sopore). [*TIME* magazine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TIME_\(magazine\)) described the incident as such: "In retaliation for the killing of one soldier, paramilitary forces rampaged through Sopore's market, setting buildings ablaze and shooting bystanders. The Indian government pronounced the event 'unfortunate' and claimed that an ammunition dump had been hit by gunfire, setting off fires that killed most of the victims."[^cite_note-Blood_Tide_Rising-517] A state government inquiry into 22 October 1993 Bijbehara killings, in which the Indian military fired on a procession and killed 40 people and injured 150, found out that the firing by the forces was 'unprovoked' and the claim of the military that it was in retaliation was 'concocted and baseless'. However, the accused are still to be punished.[^cite_note-518] In its report of September 2006, [Human Rights Watch](46530.md) stated:&#x20;

> Indian security forces claim they are fighting to protect Kashmiris from militants and Islamic extremists, while militants claim they are fighting for Kashmiri independence and to defend Muslim Kashmiris from an abusive Indian army. In reality, both sides have committed widespread and numerous human rights abuses and violations of international humanitarian law (or the laws of war).[^cite_note-HRW-519]

Many human rights organisations such as Amnesty International and Human Rights Watch (HRW) have condemned human rights abuses in Kashmir by Indians such as "extra-judicial executions", "disappearances", and torture.[^cite_note-autogenerated2-520] Bans on religious gatherings are also reportedly enforced.[^cite_note-521] The "Armed Forces Special Powers Act" grants the military, wide powers of arrest, the right to shoot to kill, and to occupy or destroy property in counterinsurgency operations. Indian officials claim that troops need such powers because the army is only deployed when national security is at serious risk from armed combatants. Such circumstances, they say, call for extraordinary measures. Human rights organisations have also asked the Indian government to repeal[^cite_note-autogenerated2007-522] the Public Safety Act, since "a detainee may be held in administrative detention for a maximum of two years without a court order."[^cite_note-autogenerated1999-523] A 2008 report by the United Nations High Commissioner for Refugees determined that [Indian Administered Kashmir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Administered_Kashmir) was only 'partly free'.[^cite_note-Amnesty.org-524] A recent report by Amnesty International stated that up to 20,000 people have been detained under a law called [AFSPA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AFSPA) in Indian-administered Kashmir.[^cite_note-autogenerated1999-523][^cite_note-autogenerated2-520][^cite_note-amnesty1997-525][^cite_note-526]

Some human rights organisations have alleged that Indian Security forces have killed hundreds of Kashmiris through the indiscriminate use of force and torture, firing on demonstrations, custodial killings, encounters and detentions.[^cite_note-527][^cite_note-amnestylibrary-528][^cite_note-529][^cite_note-530] The government of India denied that torture was widespread[^cite_note-amnestylibrary-528] and stated that some custodial crimes may have taken place but that "these are few and far between".[^cite_note-amnestylibrary-528] According to cables leaked by the WikiLeaks website, US diplomats in 2005 were informed by the International Committee of the Red Cross (ICRC) about the use of torture and sexual humiliation against hundreds of Kashmiri detainees by the security forces.[^cite_note-531] The cable said Indian security forces relied on torture for confessions and that the human right abuses are believed to be condoned by the Indian government.[^cite_note-532] SHRC also accused Indian army of forced labour.[^cite_note-533]

There have been claims of disappearances by the police or the army in Kashmir by several human rights organisations.[^cite_note-India-534] Human rights groups in Kashmir have documented more than three hundred cases of "disappearances" since 1990 but lawyers believe the number to be far higher because many relatives of disappeared people fear reprisal if they contact a lawyer.[^cite_note-535][^cite_note-536][^cite_note-537] In 2016 Jammu and Kashmir Coalition of Civil Society said there are more than 8000 forced disappearances.[^cite_note-Kashmir_Observer-515] State Human Rights Commission (SHRC) has found 2,730 bodies buried into unmarked graves, scattered in three districts — Bandipora, Baramulla, and Kupwara — of North Kashmir, believed to contain the remains of victims of unlawful killings and enforced disappearances by Indian security forces.[^cite_note-538][^cite_note-539][^cite_note-540][^cite_note-541] SHRC stated that about 574 of these bodies have already been identified as those of disappeared locals.[^cite_note-542] In 2012, the Jammu and Kashmir State government stripped its State Information Commission (SIC) department of most powers after the commission asked the government to disclose information about the unmarked graves. This state action was reportedly denounced by the former National Chief Information Commissioner.[^cite_note-543] [Amnesty International](18947898.md) has called on India to "unequivocally condemn enforced disappearances" and to ensure that impartial investigations are conducted into mass graves in its Kashmir region. The Indian state police confirms as many as 331 deaths while in custody and 111 enforced disappearances since 1989.[^cite_note-Amnesty.org-524][^cite_note-autogenerated1999-523][^cite_note-autogenerated2-520][^cite_note-amnesty1997-525] A report from the Indian Central Bureau of Investigation (CBI) claimed that the seven people killed in 2000 by the Indian military, were innocent civilians.[^cite_note-544][^cite_note-545] The Indian Army has decided to try the accused in the General Court Martial.[^cite_note-ZN-546] It was also reported that the killings that were allegedly committed in "cold-blood" by the Army, were actually in retaliation for the murder of 36 civilians \[Sikhs] by militants at Chattisingpora in 2000.[^cite_note-ZN-546] The official stance of the Indian Army was that, according to its own investigation, 97% of the reports about human rights abuses have been found to be "fake or motivated".[^cite_note-DNA-547] However, there have been at least one case where civilians have been killed in 'fake encounters' by Indian army personnel for cash rewards.[^cite_note-548] According to a report by [Human Rights Watch](46530.md):

> Indian security forces have assaulted civilians during search operations, tortured and summarily executed detainees in custody and murdered civilians in reprisal attacks. Rape most often occurs during crackdowns, cordon-and-search operations during which men are held for identification in parks or schoolyards while security forces search their homes. In these situations, the security forces frequently engage in collective punishment against the civilian population, most frequently by beating or otherwise assaulting residents, and burning their homes. Rape is used as a means of targetting women whom the security forces accuse of being militant sympathizers; in raping them, the security forces are attempting to punish and humiliate the entire community.[^cite_note-549]

The allegation of mass rape incidents as well as forced disappearances are reflected in a Kashmiri short documentary film by an Independent Kashmiri film-maker, the *[Ocean of Tears](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ocean_of_Tears)* produced by a non-governmental non-profit organisation called *the Public Service Broadcasting Trust of India* and approved by the [Ministry of Information and Broadcasting (India)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Information_and_Broadcasting_\(India\)). The film depicts mass rape incidents in [Kunan Poshpora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kunan_Poshpora) and [Shopian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shopian) as facts and alleges that Indian Security Forces were responsible.[^cite_note-550][^cite_note-551]

[Médecins Sans Frontières](20498.md) conducted a research survey in 2005 that found 11.6% of the interviewees who took part had been victims of sexual abuse since 1989.[^cite_note-552][^cite_note-553] This empirical study found that witnesses to rape in Kashmir was comparatively far higher than the other conflict zones such as Sierra Leone and Sri Lanka. 63% of people had heard of rape and 13% of the people had witnessed a rape. Dr Seema Kazi holds the security forces more responsible for raping than militants due to rape by the former being larger in scale and frequency. In areas of militant activity the security forces use rape to destroy morale of Kashmiri resistance.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKazi,_Rape,_Impunity_and_Justice_in_Kashmir201422–23-554] Dr Seema Kazi says these rapes cannot be ignored as rare occurrences nor should be ignored the documented acknowledgement of individual soldiers that they were ordered to rape.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKazi,_Rape,_Impunity_and_Justice_in_Kashmir201429-555] Kazi explains rape in Kashmir as a cultural weapon of war:

> In the particular context of Kashmir where an ethnic Muslim minority population is subject to the repressive dominance of a predominantly Hindu State, the sexual appropriation of Kashmiri women by State security forces exploits the cultural logic of rape whereby the sexual dishonour of individual women is coterminous with the subjection and subordination of Kashmiri men and the community at large.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKazi,_Rape,_Impunity_and_Justice_in_Kashmir201427-556]

Former Chief Justice of Jammu and Kashmir High Court noted in his report on human rights in Kashmir: "It is hard to escape the conclusion that the security forces who are overwhelmingly Hindu and Sikh, see it as their duty to beat an alien population into submission."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKazi,_Rape,_Impunity_and_Justice_in_Kashmir201426-557]

Some surveys have found that in the Kashmir region itself (where the bulk of separatist and Indian military activity is concentrated), popular perception holds that the Indian Armed Forces are more to blame for human rights violations than the separatist groups. Amnesty International criticized the Indian Military regarding an incident on 22 April 1996, when several armed forces personnel forcibly entered the house of a 32-year-old woman in the village of Wawoosa in the Rangreth district of Jammu and Kashmir. They reportedly molested her 12-year-old daughter and raped her other three daughters, aged 14, 16, and 18. When another woman attempted to prevent the soldiers from attacking her two daughters, she was beaten. Soldiers reportedly told her 17-year-old daughter to remove her clothes so that they could check whether she was hiding a gun. They molested her before leaving the house.[^cite_note-amnesty1997-525]

According to an [op-ed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Op-ed) published in a BBC journal, the emphasis of the movement after 1989, ″soon shifted from nationalism to [Islam](6037917.md).″ It also claimed that the minority community of [Kashmiri Pandits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kashmiri_Pandits), who have lived in Kashmir for centuries, were forced to leave their homeland.[^cite_note-BBCuk-558] Reports by the Indian government state 219 Kashmiri pandits were killed and around 140,000 migrated due to millitancy while over 3000 remained in the valley.[^cite_note-559][^cite_note-560] The local organisation of Pandits in [Kashmir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kashmir), Kashmir Pandit Sangharsh Samiti claimed that 399 Kashmiri Pandits were killed by insurgents.[^cite_note-561][^cite_note-562] Al Jazeera states that 650 Pandits were murdered by militants.[^cite_note-\:7-563] Human Rights Watch also blamed [Pakistan](23235.md) for supporting militants in Kashmir, in same 2006 report it says, "There is considerable evidence that over many years Pakistan has provided Kashmiri militants with training, weapons, funding and sanctuary. Pakistan remains accountable for abuses committed by militants that it has armed and trained."[^cite_note-HRW-519][^cite_note-564][^cite_note-565]

> Our people were killed. I saw a girl tortured with cigarette butts. Another man had his eyes pulled out and his body hung on a tree. The armed separatists used a chainsaw to cut our bodies into pieces. It wasn't just the killing but the way they tortured and killed.

— A crying old Kashmiri Hindu in refugee camps of Jammu to a BBC news reporter[^cite_note-BBCuk-558]

The violence was condemned and labelled as [ethnic cleansing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_cleansing) in a 2006 resolution passed by the [United States Congress](31756.md).[^cite_note-566] It stated that the Islamic terrorists infiltrated the region in 1989 and began an ethnic cleansing campaign to convert Kashmir into a Muslim state. According to the same resolution, since then nearly 400,000 Pandits were either murdered or forced to leave their ancestral homes.[^cite_note-USHR-567]

According to a Hindu American Foundation report, the rights and religious freedom of [Kashmiri Hindus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kashmiri_Pandit) have been severely curtailed since 1989, when there was an [organised and systematic campaign](1915235.md) by [Islamist militants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamist_terrorism) to cleanse Hindus from Kashmir. Less than 4,000 Kashmiri Hindus remain in the valley, reportedly living with daily threats of violence and terrorism.[^cite_note-HAF-568] Sanjay Tickoo, who heads the KPSS, an organisation which looks after Pandits who remain in the Valley, says the situation is complex. On one hand the community did face intimidation and violence but on the other hand he says there was no genocide or mass murder as suggested by Pandits who are based outside of Kashmir.[^cite_note-\:7-563]

The displaced Pandits, many of whom continue to live in temporary [refugee camps](438046.md) in Jammu and Delhi, are still unable to safely return to their homeland.[^cite_note-HAF-568] The lead in this act of ethnic cleansing was initially taken by the Jammu & Kashmir Liberation Front and the Hizbul Mujahideen. According to Indian media, all this happened at the instigation of Pakistan's Inter-Services Intelligence (ISI) by a group of Kashmiri terrorist elements who were trained, armed and motivated by the ISI. Reportedly, organisations trained and armed by the ISI continued this ethnic cleansing until practically all the Kashmiri Pandits were driven out after having been subjected to numerous indignities and brutalities such as rape, torture, forcible seizure of property etc.[^cite_note-RN-569]

The separatists in Kashmir deny these allegations. The Indian government is also trying to reinstate the displaced Pandits in Kashmir. Tahir, the district commander of a separatist Islamic group in Kashmir, stated: "We want the Kashmiri Pandits to come back. They are our brothers. We will try to protect them." But the majority of the Pandits, who have been living in pitiable conditions in Jammu, believe that, until insurgency ceases to exist, return is not possible.[^cite_note-BBCuk-558] Mustafa Kamal, brother of [Union Minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_Minister) [Farooq Abdullah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Farooq_Abdullah), blamed [security forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Security_Forces), former Jammu and Kashmir governor Jagmohan and PDP leader Mufti Sayeed for forcing the migration of Kashmiri Pandits from the Valley.[^cite_note-570] Jagmohan denies these allegations.[^cite_note-BBCuk-558] Pro-India politician Abdul Rashid says Pandits forced the migration on themselves so Muslims can be killed. He says the plan was to leave Muslims alone and bulldoze them freely.[^cite_note-571]

The CIA has reported that at least 506,000 people from Indian Administered Kashmir are internally displaced, about half of who are [Hindu Pandits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kashmiri_Pandits).[^cite_note-572][^cite_note-573]

### Pakistan administered Kashmir

#### Azad Kashmir

Main article: [Human rights abuses in Azad Kashmir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_rights_abuses_in_Azad_Kashmir)

The 2010 Chatham House opinion poll of Azad Kashmir's people found that overall concerns about human rights abuses in 'Azad Kashmir' was 19%.[^cite_note-\:1-512] The district where concern over human rights abuses was greatest was Bhimber where 32% of people expressed concern over human rights abuses.[^cite_note-\:1-512] The lowest was in the district of Sudanhoti where concern over human rights abuses was a mere 5%.[^cite_note-\:1-512]

Claims of religious discrimination and restrictions on religious freedom in Azad Kashmir have been made against Pakistan.[^cite_note-UNHCRpak-574] The country is also accused of systemic suppression of free speech and demonstrations against the government.[^cite_note-575][^cite_note-UNHCRpak-574] UNHCR reported that a number of Islamist militant groups, including al-Qaeda, operate from bases in Pakistani-administered Kashmir with the tacit permission of ISI[^cite_note-UNHR-576][^cite_note-UNHCRpak-574] There have also been several allegations of human rights abuse.[^cite_note-UNHR-576]

In 2006, Human Rights Watch accused ISI and the military of systemic torture with the purpose of "punishing" errant politicians, political activists and journalists in Azad Kashmir.[^cite_note-HRW2-577] According to [Brad Adams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brad_Adams), the Asia director at Human Rights Watch, the problems of human rights abuses in Azad Kashmir were not "rampant" but they needed to be addressed, and that the severity of [human rights issues in Indian-administered Kashmir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_rights_abuses_in_Jammu_and_Kashmir) were "much, much, much greater".[^cite_note-TN-578] A report titled "Kashmir: Present Situation and Future Prospects", submitted to the [European Parliament](9581.md) by [Emma Nicholson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emma_Nicholson,_Baroness_Nicholson_of_Winterbourne), was critical of the lack of human rights, justice, democracy, and Kashmiri representation in the Pakistan National Assembly.[^cite_note-579] According to the [Human Rights Commission of Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_Rights_Commission_of_Pakistan), Pakistan's ISI operates in Pakistani-administered Kashmir and is accused of involvement in extensive surveillance, arbitrary arrests, torture, and murder.[^cite_note-UNHCRpak-574] The 2008 report by the United Nations High Commissioner for Refugees determined that [Pakistani-administered Kashmir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistani-administered_Kashmir) was 'not free'.[^cite_note-UNHCRpak-574] According to Shaukat Ali, chairman of the International Kashmir Alliance, "On one hand Pakistan claims to be the champion of the right of self-determination of the Kashmiri people, but she has denied the same rights under its controlled parts of Kashmir and Gilgit-Baltistan".[^cite_note-GIC-580]

After the 2011 elections, Azad Kashmir Prime Minister Sardar Attique Ahmad Khan stated that there were mistakes in the voters list which have raised questions about the credibility of the elections.[^cite_note-ER-581]

In December 1993, the [blasphemy laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blasphemy_law) of Pakistan were extended to Pakistan Administered Kashmir. The area is ruled directly through a chief executive Lt. Gen. Mohammed Shafiq, appointed by Islamabad with a 26-member Northern Areas Council.[^cite_note-KS-582]

UNCR reports that the status of women in Pakistani-administered Kashmir is similar to that of women in Pakistan. They are not granted equal rights under the law, and their educational opportunities and choice of marriage partner remain "circumscribed". Domestic violence, forced marriage, and other forms of abuse continue to be issues of concern. In May 2007, the United Nations and other aid agencies temporarily suspended their work after suspected Islamists mounted an arson attack on the home of two aid workers after the organisations had received warnings against hiring women. However, honour killings and rape occur less frequently than in other areas of Pakistan.[^cite_note-UNHR-576]

Scholar Sumantra Bose comments that the uprising remained restricted to the Indian side and did not spill over into Pakistani-administered Kashmir despite a lack of democratic freedoms on the Pakistani side. Bose offers a number of possible explanations for this. Azad Kashmir's strong pro-Pakistan allegiances and a relatively smaller population are suggested as reasons. But Bose believes that a stronger explanation was that Pakistan had itself been a military-bureaucratic state for most of its history without stable democratic institutions. According to Bose, the Kashmiri Muslims had higher expectations from India which turned out to be a "moderately successful" democracy and it was in this context that Kashmiri Muslim rage spilled over after the rigging of the elections in 1987.[^cite_note-583] The residents of Azad Kashmir are also mostly Punjabi, differing in ethnicity from Kashmiris in the Indian administered section of the state.[^cite_note-584]

#### Gilgit-Baltistan

The main demand of the people of Gilgit-Baltistan is constitutional status for the region as a fifth province of Pakistan.[^cite_note-indian_express-585][^cite_note-gilgit_polls-586] However, Pakistan claims that Gilgit-Baltistan cannot be given constitutional status due to Pakistan's commitment to the 1948 UN resolution.[^cite_note-gilgit_polls-586][^cite_note-dawn_gilgit-587] In 2007, the [International Crisis Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Crisis_Group) stated that "Almost six decades after Pakistan's independence, the constitutional status of the Federally Administered Northern Areas (Gilgit and Baltistan), once part of the former princely state of Jammu and Kashmir and now under Pakistani control, remains undetermined, with political autonomy a distant dream. The region's inhabitants are embittered by Islamabad's unwillingness to devolve powers in real terms to its elected representatives, and a nationalist movement, which seeks independence, is gaining ground. The rise of sectarian extremism is an alarming consequence of this denial of basic political rights".[^cite_note-DAWN20090726-588] A two-day conference on Gilgit-Baltistan was held on 8–9 April 2008 at the European Parliament in [Brussels](3708.md) under the auspices of the International Kashmir Alliance.[^cite_note-thaindian10037588-589] Several members of the [European Parliament](9581.md) urged the government of Pakistan to establish democratic institutions and the rule of law in the area.[^cite_note-thaindian10037588-589][^cite_note-indianexpress556767-590]

In 2009, the Pakistani government implemented an autonomy package for Gilgit-Baltistan, which entails rights similar to those of Pakistan's other provinces.[^cite_note-indian_express-585] Gilgit-Baltistan thus gains province-like status without actually being conferred such status constitutionally.[^cite_note-indian_express-585][^cite_note-dawn_gilgit-587] Direct rule by Islamabad has been replaced by an elected legislative assembly under a chief minister.[^cite_note-indian_express-585][^cite_note-dawn_gilgit-587] The 2009 reform has not satisfied locals who demand citizenship rights and it has continued to leave Gilgit Baltistan's constitutional status within Pakistan undefined; although it has added to the self-identification of the territory. According to Antia Mato Bouzas, the PPP-led Pakistani government had attempted a compromise between its official position on Kashmir and the demands of a population where the majority may have pro-Pakistan sentiments.[^cite_note-591]

There has been criticism and opposition to this move in Pakistan, India, and Pakistani-administered Kashmir.[^cite_note-DAWN20090830-592] The move has been dubbed a cover-up to hide the real mechanics of power, which allegedly are under the direct control of the Pakistani federal government.[^cite_note-dailytimes2112010-593] The package was opposed by Pakistani Kashmiri politicians who claimed that the integration of Gilgit-Baltistan into Pakistan would undermine their case for the independence of Kashmir from India.[^cite_note-autogenerated3-594] 300 activists from Kashmiri groups protested during the first Gilgit-Baltistan legislative assembly elections, with some carrying banners reading "Pakistan's expansionist designs in Gilgit-Baltistan are unacceptable"[^cite_note-gilgit_polls-586] In December 2009, activists from nationalist Kashmiri groups staged a protest in [Muzaffarabad](1119495.md) to condemn the alleged rigging of elections and the killing of an 18-year-old student.[^cite_note-595]

## Map legality

As with other disputed territories, each government issues maps depicting their claims in Kashmir territory, regardless of actual control. Due to India's Criminal Law Amendment Act, 1961, it is illegal in India to exclude all or part of Kashmir from a map (or to publish any map that differs from those of the [Survey of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Survey_of_India)).[^cite_note-596]

## Statistics

### Government and quasi-government sources

In 2008, authorities said that 43,000 people have been killed in the violence.[^cite_note-\:12-329] Data released in 2011 by [Jammu and Kashmir government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_of_Jammu_and_Kashmir) stated that, in the last 21 years, 43,460 people have been killed in the Kashmir insurgency. Of these, 21,323 are militants, 13,226 civilians killed by militants, 3,642 civilians killed by security forces, and 5,369 policemen killed by militants.[^cite_note-597] In 2019, following the abrogation of [Article 370](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Article_370_of_the_Constitution_of_India), Home minister [Amit Shah](28165549.md) said that 35,000 security personnel had died as a result of the insurgency, and that the abrogation would serve as a tribute to their sacrifices.[^cite_note-598]

According to the Government of India Home Ministry, 2008 was the year with the lowest civilian casualties in 20 years, with 89 deaths, compared to a high of 1,413 in 1996.[^cite_note-599] In 2008, 85 security personnel died compared to 613 in 2001, while 102 militants were killed. The human rights situation improved, with only one custodial death, and no custodial disappearances. Many analysts say Pakistan's preoccupation with jihadis within its own borders explains the relative calm.[^cite_note-600] In March 2009, Abdullah stated that only 800 militants were active in the state and out of these only 30% were Kashmiris.[^cite_note-601]

### Independent, separatist, and militant sources

Since 1989 and by 2006, over 50,000 people are claimed by [Human Rights Watch](46530.md) to have died during the conflict,[^cite_note-HRW-519] with at least 20,000 of them civilian.[^cite_note-602] In 2016 [Jammu and Kashmir Coalition of Civil Society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jammu_and_Kashmir_Coalition_of_Civil_Society) said there had been 70,000 plus killings, a majority committed by the Indian armed forces.[^cite_note-Kashmir_Observer-515] The pro-Pakistan Hurriyat group has claimed a higher death toll of 80,000 including civilians, security forces and militants.[^cite_note-603] [Syed Salahuddin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syed_Salahuddin), the head of the [Hizbul Mujahideen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hizbul_Mujahideen) separatist organization, has claimed that 37,000 Indian security personnel have been killed in the militancy, "and twice that number wounded", while he put the number of militants killed at 15,000.[^cite_note-604]

## Natural disaster diplomacy

The [2005 Kashmir earthquake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2005_Kashmir_earthquake), which killed over 80,000 people, led to India and Pakistan finalising negotiations for the opening of a road for disaster relief through Kashmir.[^cite_note-605] [2014 India–Pakistan floods](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_India%E2%80%93Pakistan_floods) was also followed by statements of cooperation by leaders of both countries.[^cite_note-606][^cite_note-607][^cite_note-608][^cite_note-609]

[^cite_note-Korbel506-126]: &#x20; [Korbel (1953](#CITEREFKorbel1953), pp. 506–507): "When a further Security Council resolution urged the governments of India and Pakistan to agree within thirty days on the demilitarization of Kashmir, on the basis of Dr. Graham's recommendation, Pakistan once more accepted and India once more refused....Dr. Graham met the Indian request for retaining in Kashmir 21,000 men, but continued to propose 6,000 soldiers on the Azad side. Pakistan could not accept the first provision and India continued to insist on its stand concerning the Azad forces. The meeting, which ended in failure, was accompanied by bitter comments in the newspapers of both India and Pakistan about United Nations intervention in the Kashmir dispute."

[^cite_note-128]: &#x20; [Korbel (1953](#CITEREFKorbel1953), p. 507): "With the hindsight of six years, the Council's approach, though impartial and fair, appears to have been inadequate in that it did not reflect the gravity of the Kashmir situation.... The Security Council did not deal with either of these arguments \[India's assumption of the legal validity of the accession and Pakistan's refusal to recognize its validity]. Nor did it consider the possibility of asking the International Court of Justice for an advisory opinion on the juridical aspect of the conflict under Article 96 of the Charter. Nor did it invoke any provisions of Chapter VII of the Charter, which deals with 'acts of aggression'." [Subbiah (2004](#CITEREFSubbiah2004), p. 180): "From the beginning, the Security Council framed the problem as primarily a political dispute rather than looking to a major legal underpinning of the dispute: the Instrument of Accession's validity or lack thereof."

[^cite_note-146]: &#x20; Choudhury, Golam (1968). [*Pakistan's Relations with India: 1947–1966*](https://archive.org/details/pakistansrelatio0000chou). Praeger. pp. [178](https://archive.org/details/pakistansrelatio0000chou/page/178). Indian leaders...continued to express the hope that partition would ultimately be undone; in particular they envisaged the possibility of annexing East Pakistan. Pakistan's resentment...was confined to a disputed area...when as a result of Indian intransigence the prospects of a peaceful solution of the Kashmir issue seemed bleak, there were outbursts of anti-Indian feelings in Pakistan...Alleged talk of 'holy war' or *Jehad* referred to the disputed territory of Kashmir. But in India, leaders, press and even scholars had no hesitation in expressing the hope of undoing the partition and thus annihilating Pakistan. Choudhury, Golam (1968). [*Pakistan's Relations with India: 1947–1966*](https://archive.org/details/pakistansrelatio0000chou). Praeger. pp. [175](https://archive.org/details/pakistansrelatio0000chou/page/175). Most of those quotations related to the period after the signing of the Liaquat-Nehru Agreement of April 8, 1950 under which India and Pakistan undertook not to permit propaganda in either country...seeking to incite war between the two countries. The government of Pakistan initiated twenty-seven complaints of flagrant violation of the Agreement by a number of influential Indian newspapers, but no effective action was taken by the Indian government, the plea being that its scope for action was limited by the India constitution. The Pakistan government pointed out that, if this were the position, the government of India should not have undertaken an international obligations which it was not in a position to carry out. The government of India made only eight complaints about alleged violation of the Agreement. Choudhury, Golam (1968). [*Pakistan's Relations with India: 1947–1966*](https://archive.org/details/pakistansrelatio0000chou). Praeger. pp. [166](https://archive.org/details/pakistansrelatio0000chou/page/166). Liaquat drew attention to the continuous and blatant propaganda for war against Pakistan, and indeed for the very liquidation of Pakistan, carried on by the Indian press, prominent leaders and political parties which openly adopted as an article of creed the undoing of partition.- which meant nothing but liquidation of Pakistan. No doubt there had been talk of Jehad or liberation of the Muslim population of Kashmir in Pakistan but...Pakistan's grievances have always been confined to Kashmir which...is a disputed territory. It was wrong to construe expressions giving vent to feelings of frustration over the failure of peaceful methods of solution in Kashmir as a desire for war against India. But, in India, the creation of Pakistan itself is still regarded as a tragic mistake which ought to be corrected.

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMetcalfMetcalf2006p._273:_"The_year_1989_marked_the_beginning_of_a_continuing_insurgency,_fuelled_by_covert_support_from_Pakistan._The_uprising_had_its_origins_in_Kashmiri_frustration_at_the_state’s_treatment_by_Delhi._The_imposition_of_leaders_chosen_by_the_centre,_with_the_manipulation_of_local_elections,_and_the_denial_of_what_Kashmiris_felt_was_a_promised_autonomy_boiled_over_at_last_in_the_militancy_of_the_Jammu_and_Kashmir_Liberation_Front,_a_movement_devoted_to_political,_not_religious,_objectives."-20]: &#x20;[Metcalf & Metcalf 2006](#CITEREFMetcalfMetcalf2006), p. 273: "The year 1989 marked the beginning of a continuing insurgency, fuelled by covert support from Pakistan.
